SIR G. 13Esnalogr once met Quin at a very small dinner party. There was a delicious pudding, which the master of the house, pushing the dish towards Quin, begned him to taste. A gentleman had just before helped himself to an immense piece of it. "Pray," said Quin, looking first at the gentleman s plate and then at the dish, "what is the pudding?" NEVER VARIED.—A story is told to the effect that a young wernan who, giving evidence in a court, on being asked her age and replying that she was 30, was asked if she had not given the same age when before the magistrate a couple of _years previously. " CAPITAL" CHAT.

Mr. Austen Chamberlain has furnistied the first Parliamentary smile of the present week. Things were very dull on Monday, until question Mo. 14, standing in the name of Mr. Chamberlain, was reached. His son promptly rose and reinarked,'. " On behalf of my right honorable relative, I beg to ask question Ne.l4:' This variation from the last occasion of the kind, when young Chamberlain referred to the author of his being as " my right honorable friend," made the whole house smile, the Speaker included. Had the incident occurred a week or two ago, when the benches were well filled, the smile would in all probability have 'developed into a roar. But at the fag end of the session,with considerably more membets absent than present., it is not so easy to generate a good healthy laugh.

The nearest approach to a good laugh was reached at a late stage, when business was temporarily sus- pended for a moment or two while. Mr. Bowles and Mr. Banbury were engaged in consultation as to the next move in the game of obstruction. Mr. Bowles was understood to be addressing the House at the ,time ; nevertheless, he stopped short, and, ignoring the Chair, conversed in undertones , with his neigri- bour, Mr. Banbury, in the coolest and most nonchal- ant fashion imaginable. The cynical audacity. 01 the proceeding tickled most of the members present and provoked ironical cheers, blended with subdued laughter, from the Radical and Irish benches.

The indecent attack made on Mr. Gladstone from the pulpit by a Scotch Episcopalian clergyman on 6uhday last has its parallel in Ireland. Shortly aftet the rejection of the Home Rule Bill in 1886 a well-known Irish Protestant Nationalist attended a harvest thanksgiving service in a church in Dublin. The preacher enumerated amongst the blessings lately vouchsafed to the Irish Protestants their pre- servation from the traitorous designs of wicked and , disaffected men to sever Ireland from Great Britain I and to institute a regime of bitter religious persecu- tion. -- Lotd Decies did not follow example of the old Duke of Queensbury, who gave his doctor a good salary while he was kept alive, but not a penny on iiis death. Lord Decies has left his doctor two hundred guineas a year. This eccentric peerewho lately died, never took the trouble to claim his vote in the House of Lords. He was described at his death as having " lived in retirement" for seven years at the North Eastern Hotel in York. A curious place for retirement, a busy station hotel !

" On Hot Rolls to Fame I" would be a good title for a book giving the life and history of Major ktopner, who is the generous giver of the public park to the town of Stockton-on-Tees, which is -oon to be opened by the Duke and Duchess ol York. Major Ropier began life as a German baker,l and 25 years ago was selling hot lolls of a morningl in the streets of Hartlepool. He is now one of the Largest and wealthiest shipowners and ship builders in the North of England, and the value of the park lie is giving to the public is no less than £lO.OOO. [he major is an officer of the Ist Durham Rifle Volunteers, and lives like a prince at Preston Hall, near Stockton. He is a prominent member of the South Durham Hunt, and his near neighbour at VVynyard, Lord Londonderry, has been instrumen- tal in getting the consent of their Royal Highnesses to open the park.

The list of aristocratic tradesmen goes on increas- tug. Hear are a few more that have been un- earthed. The Austrian Prime Minister, Count Taafe, who is also a British peer, is making a hand- ome profit from the sale of Ellischan cheeses made on his estate ; Count Harracb, one ot the most it:- timate friends of the late Austrian Crown Prince, owes the principal part of his incomi to the manu- facture ot Bohemian glass ; the Prince of Schwart- zenberg are brewers, but even the fortune 'made out of beer is not sufficient to keep up the extravagance of the head ot the house ; the Princes of Lobkowitz have their fine old name on boxes of patent medicines; while, as everyone knows, Prince Bismark sells farm produce, and grumbles at the prices.

There is a good deal of interest in the House as to the whereabouts oi W. H. Hurlbert, and ques- tions are to be put to the Government on the subject on Monday night. It is not for a moment believed that he is at Genoa, although the letters from him which have been sent to the newspapers bear. I hear, the postmark of that town. Still, there is a belief that he is somewhere in the Old W0..1d, and quite possibly in Italy or Greece, and there is Lnuch anxiety lest the Public Prosecutor should be lax in his efforts to solve the mystery.

The strongly worded resolution adopted by the Welsh Baptists on the Disestablishment question received considerable attention amongst members of Parliament on Wednesday. In effect it seems to be identical in policy with that carried by the Welsh members at their meeting. It does not con- template immediate secession from the Liberal party,but it urges the Welsh party to take an inde- pendent course in the session of 1894 unless the bill is taken earnestly in hand. The reports of the meeting at which this recommendation was made to the Welsh representatives have served to impress Liberal members of Parliament with a sense at the determined and rather rebellious attitude of the Welsh people. They realise thoroughly that Wales means business.

Considering the length of time that the dahlia has been in cultivation, it is perhaps the least satisfactory of flowers. It has,in fact, been spoiled as many other florist flowers have been. Prizes are given for its faults and not its possible excellences. It has no wantonness or caprice of shape ; it hurls, so to speak, its crude and glaring colours at you. A great show of dahlias was opened on Wednesday at the Earl's Court Exhibition, and among the mountains of flowers as interesting as cannon balls painted crimson there are just a few that give one hopes of more artistic culture. The hopes seem to lie principally in the direction of the new star dahlias with partly folded petals, but at present these are too severely regular, and look like mai- ! I tary decorations in metal work. A solitary single dahlia, shown by Messrs. Paul and Sons, of grey- white, with rosy blushes, has more of the beauties which dahlias in general lack than any other flower in the show. It is a new variety, and is called after the Duchess of Albany. There are many other flowersin the show, and Mr. Sleythes, gar- dener to the Duke of Northumberland at bion House takes the first prize for a group of blossoms (many orchids) and foliage plants.

Dr. Donald Macleod, who played the chief part at the new Crathie Church ceremony before the Queen is net the great doctor, but a relative. The Queen' 8 .otch affections have ever run in families, yet she h s never found anyone to fill the place left vacant when Dr. Norman Macleod, the famous minister of that barony, died. Glasgow acquaintances who had the doctor for a guest, and especially ladies, used to bombard him at dinner with questions as to what happened when be was dining with the Queen at this or that portion of the meal. One especially persistent questioner quite exhausted the doctor's power of narration, for after meetingther demands for information without flinching,be at last pleaded " Madam, it was just at this stage that her Majesty ~aid to me • Norman, will ye hae a dram,' and I said, With pleasure, your Majesty.'

No one among the ministers of the Established Church in Scotland is more popular at Balmoral that Dr. James Macgregor, the most eloquent 01 Edinburgh preachers. Dr. Macgregor has not the advantage of ' stately presence which Dr. , Normsn Macleod and Dr. Guthrie enjoyed. He is 1 slightly deformed, and so small of stature that he is called " the wee minister." One pulpit he occupied was so big that he had to stand on tip-toe to see over the edge, and as no thoughtful elder came with 1 a footstool, he gave out the psalm, "Lord, from the depths to Thee I cry." Another story told by him- self is that ot a visit to a farmhouse to see some membera of his congregation, then a rural one, who Iwere not at home. A boy about the farm, who did not know the visitor, took him round the steading, ; and, pointing out a curtosly-shaped pig, said, " We ca' him Bowdy Macgregor, after oor minister."

THE EXAMINER. SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 16, 1893 LADIES' LETTER.

DEAu Masan,

Plain bodices, whether of tweed or fancy woollens of the light summery type, may be altered for autumn wear by removing the light trimmings of lace and adding velvet pouffs, or entire sleeves and frills, or Victorian revers across the shoulders; or ribbon velvet or bias bands are charming. A combination of tan-coloured canvas or crepon with moss.green velvet makes a charming costume .in this style; Or black or petunia velvet on grey, heliotrope, or fawn crOpon is extrernsly pretty.

Some smart tailor costumes for young ladies in their teens are composed of serge or tweed. They are made with straight skirts, which can be either trimmed or not, according to fancy, with rows of woollen braid ties round them in hoops. The bodice may be made in either of two ways; the first is tight fitting with the shortest of basques, If such it can be called, for the bodice is really cat short immediately below the waist line. It is shaped en cceur at the neck, where it is finished with a turned-down collar and wide pointed revers; the right front fastens across on the left side over the rever with three large buttons. The second bodice is cut in the Eton jacket shape, barely long enough to reach the waist ; it is finished in the same way as the fitting corsage, with 8. turned-down collar and revers, and is worn over a full silk blouse. The sleeves of both bodices are cut in a coat shape, with an ample fulness upon the shoulder.

A styllsh•looking mantle is of tweed, water- proofed with indiarubber. The cape is detachable, and can be worn separately. If preferred, a plain tweed can be had instead of a check.

We are likely to have plain skirts, but very flowing ones, this winter in rich materials, such as velvets, dpingle, or moire antique. These will be trimmed down the front with handsome jet plaques or ornaments placed at intervals. Pelisses will bo much worn, and a forerunner of these is the new dust cloak, made in alpaca, pong.L., shot silk, or mery satin. The back is made ea princesse, and the fronts have one curved but very loose side seam, the cloak fastening on one side with three rosettes of the silk. A very wide revers collar is edged with a deep fall of 6cru or black lace. Fancy blouse bodices are pretty as well as economical garments, and truly they are the rage. New designs for making them come out constantly, so that we are not at a loss for variety. Cotton blouses always look very fresh in the summer, and are much more practical when worn with serge skirts than entire dresses composed of washing materials. These blouses will be repeated for home wear during the autumn and coming winter in fancy flannel and other woollen materials. The former can now be had in many different colours and patterns. Those spotted with black or white spots and rings make up very prettily. Silk blouses should of course be reservld for the after- noon, while those which are made of cheaper materials save many a better frock, as they can be used with half-worn skirts.

Capes will be very popular for wearing until really cold weather sets in, and they are being made in a great variety of shapes and sizes. Just now the most fashionable kind seems to be a small shoulder cape of velvet, with a deep frill of coarse ecru lace tacked beneath it so as to give the effect of a double cape, the neck being finished with a toll lace ruff fastening in front under a velvet rosette. This is a very smart little wrap, and can be made at home withes r, much difficulty if a little care is taken in cutting. The cape is not very wide, and what fulness there is is arranged in box. pleats, two in front and two at the back, the part over the shoulders being plain. Some of the newest three-quarter capes are of moire, with deep falling epaulettes of velvet, edged with narrow fur, but in every case the shoulder-pieces are so cut as to give a sloping effect, and a full ruche of velvet or lace is invariably substituted for the high collar of last winter. The newest jackets, which are, however, as I have hinted, not much worn yet, have rather short, full basques and long pelerine fronts, and are much trimmed.

Your ever loving, Jumns.

The German Empress is a blonde with one little weak- ness—for surroundings of pale blue. Women should see that the tedious and old-fashioned word "lady "is eliminated from their titles. So says an advanced woman journalist. The Queen of Greece is president of a sisterhood devoted to the reformation of criminals, and she person- ally visits prisoners. The only ornament ever worn by the widowed Arch- duchess Stephanie, is a locket containing the portrait of her little daughter on one side and that of her mother, the Queen of the Belgians, on the other. The Empress of Austria has to give a written receipt for the State jewels every time she wears them, and her Majesty as a result, usually contents herself with her private collection, which is worth £300,000. Mrs. G. S. Heaney. the author of so many children's tales, has recently written a novel appealing to older readers. The title has not yet been definitely decided. It will be issued in three volumes by Messrs. Bliss, Sands and Foster next month. The Queen insists on all her small grandsons wearing Highland dress when they are with her ; and even the little girls of the Duke o! Connaught wear plaid kilts, Scotch caps, and short jakets when they are under their Royal Grandmamma's eye. Miss Macintyre the well-known vocalist, was born In India. In four years she has reached a high summit in public favour. Her mother acts as her secretary, and

tho arrangement of her numerous engagements occupies much time. Miss Macintyre was lucky enough to sing in Dr. Liszt's oratorio, " St. Elizabeth," when tle,e vet- eran composer was visiting London, and hisapproffation sealed her success. • To the ailing about Sandringham the Princess of Wales devotes much time and makes many sacrifices. "Sir," says a tenant of 30 years standing. " I have known the Royal lady leave a sick labourer's bedside at ten o'clock at night, go to her own home, take delicate things from her own dinner tab:e, and brint them back herself to the sick man at nearly eleven o'clock at night. How many ladies would do as much for the poor 2" 1 While on this subject it may be .as well to note that it *i in my own knowledge that up to a very few years ago Her Majesty continued her singing, preserving her 'voice and execution, and • used to sing duets with the Princess Beatrice, who wail very carefully taught, fias (Pike a good voice, and sings with great feeling and taste. Lady Florence Dixie, acclrrling to the "North British Mail," was offered £135,000 by a millionaire Well known in London slcietv,to start a halfpenny daily Woman's paper. Lady Florence, however, has declined On the ground that the millionaire wanted it a fashion- able woman's organ, whereas,she wanted to conduq it on advanced women suffrage lines. Only two ladies seem to have hitherto received the fkeeclum of London City. One is the Baroness Burdett- Coutts, the ,other is Lady Charlotte Schrieber. Lady Charlotte owes her distinction to the Company of the Fantnakers. She is the greatest living authority on fans of all kinds, British or Foreign, ancient or'inodern, and spent over 20 years in collecting the 350 rare fans she presentea to the British Museum. . " The ugliest women in the world " are the cleverest, according to Sir Crichton Browne. He fears that what woman'. gains intellectually by the higher education now in vogue, she will lose in beauty and grace, and often in health, too. Amongst the Garo nation, a peo- ple dwelling on a range of hills between the Bralunapoo- tra. and the Soorma valleys, the women are supreme. They woo the men, they control the affairs of the home and the nation, property descends through them, and in everything they are d •ininent, but—note the sequel-- they are the very ugliest women on the face of the earth.

The Queen as a Singer. Some paragraphs appeared in this column says a writer in a London contemporary calling attention to the singularity of so good a musician as the Duke of Edinburgh succeeding such a successful composer as the late Duke Ernest of Saxe-Coburg-Gotha. These have been repeated by a contemporary who, by the way of making them appear original, has added the startling statement that" the musical tastes and talents which all the Queen's children possess are derived from their lather," and then he goes on to say that H,er Majesty "has never laid claim to anything more than an appre- ciation of music and its charms." This is sheer non- sense, for, though the Queen may not " lay claim to anything' more than an appreciation," site is an exceed- ingly accomplished performer, well-posted student of music, and used to sing with great precision and taste, having a particulaily sweet voice. - - -

My judgment on this-point might be disputed, but few will gainsay ' Mendelssohn's sight to speak with authority. In a private letter -written from Frankfort on July 19th, 1842, to his mother, he quite ingenuously describes a very charming scene in the vie intime of the. Roy al pair. It is too long to quote in its entirety, but one bit of it I will give. Alter a long but interesting openihg, he says:—" The Duchess of Kent came in, too and while. they were all talking I rummaged about amongst the music and soon discovered my first set of songs. So, of course, I begged her rather to sing one ot those than the Gluck, to which she very kindly con- sented ; and which did she chocse 2—Schoner und schoner schmuckt sich !—sang it quite charmingly in strict time and tune, and with very good execution. Only in the line ' Der prosa lasten und mull' where it goes down to D and then comes up again by semi-tones, she sang D sharp each time,and as I gave her the note the two first times the last time she sang D where it ought to have been D sharp. But with the exception of this little mistake it was really charming, and the last long G I have never heard better or purer or more natural from any amateur. Then I was obliged to confess that Fanny had written the song (which I found very hard, but pride must have a fall), and to beg her to sing one of mine also. This indisputable evidence of the Queen's prowess, tempered as it oby just criticism, goes on as follows :—" If I would give her plenty of help she would gladly try, she said, and then she sang the Pilger- spruch Lass dich nur ' really quite faultlessly, and with charming feeling and expression. I thought to myself one must not pay too many compliments on such an occasion, en I merely thanked her a great many times, upon which she said, ' Oh, if I had not been so frightened; generally I have such long breath.' Then I praised her heartily, and with the best conscience in the world ; for just that part with the long C at the close she had done so well, taking it and three notes next to it all in the some breath as one seldom hears it done, and there- fore it ammied me doubly that she herself shoul.i hayç begun about it."

Position for Healthy Sleep. A doctor writing in a medical paper says, "An im- mense number of people sleep on the left side, and this is the most common cause of the unpleasant taste in the mouth in the morning which is generally attributed to dyspepsia. If a meal has been taken within two or three hours of going to bed, to sleep on the left side is to give I the stomach a task which is difficult in the extreme to I perform. The student of anatomy knows that all food leaves the stomach on the right side, and hence sleeping cn the left side soon after eating involves a sort of pump- ing operation which is anything but conducive to sound repose. The action of the heart is also interfered with considerably, and the lungs are unduly compressed. It lis probable that lying on the back is the most natural position, but few men can rest easily so, and hence it is best to cultivate the habit of sleeping on the right side."

-- Our Eyesight. It is stated that near sight is more frequent in cities than in the country ; for in the city it is not possible to use the eye for far distances, and it is therefore insuffi- ciently exercised. Sailors, who are always observing distant objects, are rarely, if ever, short-sighted. A had light is a proved and frequent cause of near sight. Our rooms should be well lighted, but the light should not dazzle the eyes. When we read,it is best for the light to fall over the left shoulder ; it is injurious to come from the front. It should be steady, and any flickering, such as is usually seen in candle flames, end occasionally in bad gas burners, is very trying. A but position of the body while reading favours the development of near sight. The page ought to be nearly perpendicular to the line of sight,and should be held about 15in, from the eye ; the body should be kept as upright as possible while reading or writing, and not—as is often the case— held down over the page. Lying down while reading is injurious. Reading in bed by the light of a candle is , especially to be condemned. Another cause of near sight is the persistent disregard of hygienic require- ments—studs ing in ill-ventilated rooms, in overheated or cold rooms, want of outdoor exercise. Closely as- sociated with the last mentioned cause is ill-health of every kind, more especially gastric derangements. Lastly, over-fatigue of the eyes, reading very fine print, sewing by twilight, or using the eyes when they are aching from gazing at fire light or snow, may also induce short sight.

Find this girl for use, Retro, and 1 will give you ten thousand /mends !" He spoke slowly and impressively, watching the valet like a lynx. . ;Pietro's features quivered for an instant. That's a large sum of money for the finding of a poor governess," said the man. "A large sum ; but I will double it ! Find this girl, and put her in my keeping, Pietro, and. I.will give you twenty thousand pounds, !" . :The Maltese drew his breath more quickly. The reward tempted him sorely. .His captive had,hald out most : unexpectedly, and with the utmost bravery, against starvation, solitude, darkness, and terrors innumerable. He had not Seen her for two ifights, and he hoped that when lie should visit her again, she would consent to marry him. Bat what if she should still refuse? What if she should really die rather than to accept freedom as his wife? Why, then he should lose her, the fortune he had . expected to OM' through her, and this sum Which Lord Darkwood offered him for her discovery. Would it not be wise to accept the sum now offered, him and give up the girl to her enemy ? Lord Darkwood noted his hesitation and-caught , ab it wills eagernesi. His lordship began to believe ! that the valet might know some:hing after all of Gwendoline. "Think what you can do with twenty thousand :pounds, Pietro he said, insinuatingly. "I will decide to-night what I will do. 'lll conclude to take up this search and earn the stun--you-offer me,- I will tell you in the morning." . •• , :Why net decide to-night ?" "I have no time. Ho retired from the room. "That rascal will bear watching,!" the marquis muttered. "If he had suspected her identity he might have been, the cause of her disappeatanee. But there was her letter 1 She went off of her own accord—the letter proves that. Yet he may have some suspicion of her refuge. I'll urge him anew in the morning. And I'll watch him! 'Curse him 1 rzu uneasy !" And the more he thought the more uneasy ho

grew. Pietro glided along the hall, and as he aseenile the stairs the butler extinguished the light in the state apartments, and made rmldy for the adven- ture be had in bawl. lie had found opportunity during the evening to secure the keys ot the ruins, and these were now upon his person. He bad secured all the doors, and it was now close upon twelve o'clock. His duties were ended until the morrow. As Pietro went up to his room, the butler, Morris, let himself out into the grounds by a private door, which he locked behind him. He knew that Mr. Sutton had gone out an hour before, and was wan- dering about the g,ounds. He kept in the shadow of the trees, and passed around lo the vicinity of the ruins. At the little door set deep in the stone wall, he found a little group awaiting him—Miss Norreys, Lord Chilton, Mr. Sutton, Mr. Barsby, and the Hindoo, Aga. Mr. Barsby had brought a couple of lanterns, and the butler was similarly provided. The lanterns were distributed, and Morris unlocked the door, and they crept softly into the ruins. "I know the way, said Morris, "every step of it. Follow me 1" He led them through passages and rooms to the staircase that led below, unlocking such doors as were locked. They descended the stair and stood in the grim stone corridor below. It was frightfully dark, and the light of the lan- tern seemed to render the surrounding darkness more dense by contrast. "I feel like a burglar," said Mr. Barsby, ner- vously. "I hope that we are not getting ourselves into some scrape. I presume Lord Darkwood could send us to prison for this midnight entrance into his premises. "I will take all the risk," said Miss Norreys. " Come I Let us hasten towards the farthest dun- geous 1"

CHArnm Lxim

Miss Norreys led the way in her eagerness. They traversed passage after passage, guided by Morris, whose remembrance of these subterranean corridors were not so vivid as he had declared. They made one or two wrong turnings, and Morris at last confessed that he was not quite sure which passage next to take. They paused in dismay. "Hark !" whispered Miss, Norreyis, suddenly, growing white as death. Hide the lights I Quick I" They obeyed her on the instant. The next moment they beheld, turning into the same corridor in which they found themselves, a man bearing a lantern and a basket. This was Pietro! *They, crouched against the wall, silent as the grave, until he was far in advance of them. Mute as the dead, but with hearts beating like drums, they crept after him. He turned into another passage—they also turned at the same point, a little later. It was well that they had been cautious, for Pietro had paused only a few feet distant, and was unlocking a door. He opened it; took a jug of water and a loaf of bread from his basket, and carried them into the cell. The faces of those who followed him flamed at the sight. They crept closer—to the very door and peered in! Ah, what was that they saw? A grim and horrible cell—Pietro standing with demon-like exultation on his visage the lantern held above his head—and, crouching in an arm- chair, a slight and wasted figure in deep shadow. They held their breaths. The little figure in the , chair rose up and crept into the light. Wan and ! Lord .Darkwood stood up and 'stared at her with singular agitation. His hair and beard had been trimmed since she had seen him, apd he looked handsome and noble, and years younger than in the morning. "Madam," he said, "I am come to offer you my thanlcs." "No thanks are needed," said the heiress, coldly. "My lord, I have but carried to, its fulfilment a vow I made years ago to be revenged upon two scoundrels. One lies dead in Dunholm ; the other is here I" . "Madam—" "Let me tell you the story," said the lady rapidly, not noticing that the door had opened and a stranger—grim Old squire Markham—stood upon the threaliold, surveying the scene, and regarding her with dilating eyes. "Eighteen years ago' you and your cousin were introduced to an English girl, in the house of an English friend in Germany. You both professed to love her. She refused Captain Tollish. She accepted you, my lord. She was a wayward ,girl—the spoiled darling of a father's heart. You professed fear of your stern -. old father, you persuaded the girl to marry you, and secretly. Is not this so ?" "It is; but— ", "The girl loved you. ..She knew that her doting old father had a weakness for titles and rank. She believed that he would be pleated with her mar- riage, when it should be declared. She thought of him—yes,. even in her passion and wilfulness, she thought of him I" cried Miss Norreys, vehemently. "And she did not mean to wound him. She con- sented to the marriage. You, my lord, made Captain Tollish your confidant, not knowing that. he had been the lover of this girl, and you solicited his services in bringing about the marriage. You commissioned hint to procure an English minister and a licence. He did both. One evening, when the family had gong out, and the girl was left alone in her friends' house, you came with the minister, your cousin, and the licence, and you and she were married." "Madam !" cried his lordship, in a voice of agony. She continued, not heeding him— 'After the marriage, you persuaded her to go, away with you. You took her to Italy, and stayed there with her for months. She grieved to see her ' father. Conscience stung her cruelly. She cried for her dear father-, who must believe ill things of her. And at last, my lord, her tears and entreaties induced you to conse,nt to see your father and acknowledge your marriage to the world. You brought your young wife to England. You estab- lished her in lodgings at the West-end. You were known as Mr. afid Mrs.. Charteris. Weeks and months passed. Your wife pined to see her dear old father's face. Often her pillow -was wet with her tears. You had been married nearly a year when the end came." Lord Darkwood uttered an imploring cry. She still paid no heed to him, saying rapidly— " You went out one day to see your father and Implore his forgiveness and his recognition of your wife. In your absence a carriage drove up to the door, and two gentlemen alighted. One of these was Captain Tollish. The other was a grey-haired, aristocratic man, your father. They asked for Mrs. Charteris, and were shown up to her room. Then Lord Darkwood introduced himself, and said that be had come to put an end to his son's dis- reputable connection with a shameless woman. Your wife cried out that she was married, and your father sneered at her. 'Do the heirs of s. marquis wed with the daughters of Yorkshire squires?" he asked... In an agony she showed her marriage certificate. "'What of that?' cried Captain Tollish, with the sneer of a demon. 'lt was I who procured. the licence and the minister. You remember, madam, that.' proffered you an honourable mar- riage, but you rejected me. I vowed to be revenged upon you. Well, I have had my revenge.'" The girl turned sick and giddy. What could he mean I And then he told her that he had never intended that she should become the wife of his cousin, and that the minister he had procured was no minister, but an actor of low character who had been hired for the purpose." "The girl refused to believe it. She raved like a mad creature. Was it for this she had given up her dear old father? She dared Captain Tollish to the proof. He showed her a letter from the actor , who had performed the mock ceremony. He gays he further proofs. The old marquis joined in, and told her that she was no wile, that she was a lost creature, and that her husband had learned the truth and deserted her. Her husband—for was he not that in the sight of heaven? --had tired of his lower-born young wife.. and had taken advantage of the flaw IA thiii'Marrialie; and yielded to his father's persuasions and given her up. "They proved it to her. And then I think she went mad! She plucked the wedding-ring from her finger and flung it into the old lord's face I She seized a bonnet and shawl from a sofa, ttirew them upon her, and dashed from the house! And Nitain Tollish's sneering laugh rang out after her she flew in frenzy out into the street:

gon Car'

"I cannot tell you all that happened next," said Miss Norreys, wearily. "The girl was mad. Her lover had been false to her. He had given her up, when every principle of honour and decency would have compelled him to offer an immediate marriage. Ho had abandoned her! Her brain was on fire! Her one thought was now of her dear old father, of her home, of her old chamber, with its outlook on the moors. To see her father's face again—and die ! That was her prayer. "She wandered through country roads and lanes, thinking always of her father. Sometimes she got a ride with some good farmer. No one ever insulted her. Her madness protected her ! On—on—to the northward I Always with the thought of her father in her heart, the dear old father, who, if all the world should forsake her, would still cling to lier, still love her I" Squire Markham, just within the door, gave a frenzied groan. No one heeded him. Every eye was fixed upon that pale olive face, lit up by glow- ing orbs like suns. "Did the girl reach her home ?" said Miss Nor- ' reys, slowly, falteringly. "There was a storm—a waste like her native moors—the old house! was perhaps like a dream. The father met her .at the door with a curse. But her old nurse received her and cared for her. Under the roof beneath which she had woke to life her child was born. Then came the agony, the shame, and, mingled with it, the lingering madness. She felt no love for the child of the man who had abandoned her. But she felt sorry for it. She was hopeless utterly hopeless. Why had she not died? But strength came back to her body, and she mast live. 0 There was no escape for her—none "One night she heard the old nurse pleading with her father to forgive her. And he cursed her He cursed his only child, and said she must begone I from his house. She was left alone. Her madness came upon her more fiercely. She stole out into the night, the storm, the darkness. She wandered , on the moor—she staggered—she fell And so she died! Mv lord, you were freed from her. On the wild Yo 'hire waste, the girl you fancied for twelvemonth was your wife, and whom you dis- carded, died. I swore when I heard the terrible story to avenge her. Have I not done it? Captain Tollish is stripped of his possessions, and 'heaven i has punished him with death. Have I not revenge& that girl upon you, my lord? I have given you back your freedom, your title, wealth, home.-- everything. I have heaped coals of fire upon your , head! My task is done! I leave Beechm Ant this very night. Go home, my lord, and think of the girl you destroyed—and who is dead." She moved toward the door. Lord Darkwood, with ashen face and wild eye!, - : ran after her. She halted. Then, opening wiue his arms, he called to her in a voice of agony— Listen to me !" , She shook her head, and essayed to move on. "Listen to my defence !" She looked at him with eyes of fire—black now; ! with a strange red gleam in them, and he retreatett •I a step, and cried again, in a voice strained with anguish, wild with incredulity— ' " Clare ! rang through those dungeons like a wild alarm, he dashed into the cell, knocked Pietro over as if he had been a wooden image, and gathered Gwen up into his arils in an agony of tenderness and yearn- ing. • Pietro picked himself up in a panic, and dashed out at The door, to find himself a prisoner in the bands of Mr. Sutton and the Hindoo. He glared at them like a maniac, _ _

MisiNorreys rushed into the dungeon and with a look that compelled Lord Chilton's obedience, so wild was it with imploring, took Gwen from his arms. She, kissed' he sweet face, now rigid in a swoon, chafed the hands, and called upon the girl to awaken in tones piteous enough to call back the dead. •

When Gwen returned to cOnsciousness, it was to find her laver and Miss Norreys bending over her, both.tender and full of passionate love. She gave a hand to each and shuddered as she glanced ,around her with a look of terror.

"You are safe, darling," said the viscount. "Oh, heaven, what you must have suffered !" "You are safe now, darling," repeated ' Miss Norreys. "I San ready to die now. But, no, we have more to do still." _

_ "There is another prisoner,' said Gwen, feebly. ," In a lower dungeon. Pietro has the key. There it is attached to the key in the lock." Miss Norreys sprang forward and snatched the keys. "Let us lose no time," she cried. "Bring Gwen and come."

Mr. Sutton and, Mr. Bursby had expressed their horror of Gwen's imprisonment, and had shaken hands with her in token of their delight at lier rescue. Then Lord Chilton:took his betrothed in his arms—ah, how light she was—as if he meant never to let her go again. Mr. Barsby and the Hindoo marched Pietro between them, and Miss Norreys ran on in advance side by side by the now half-frenzied. Morris. . .

They descended some steps and paused at a locked door. They opened it, and out sprang a mail wild of eye and aspect, with long, fair beard flowing on his breast, and long hair clrcoping on his shoulders—a man whose ghastly pallor told of long imprisonment. • But, despite his pallor and wildness, his stately. carriage remained, lie was noble in his appearance, with a grand and halighty head, and solitude; and darkness, and all•the horrors of his long imprison- ment had not cowed his spirit. "At last !" he said, slowly. "At last !" and now his voice took an exultant tone. "At last 1 I thank Thee, oh God."

CHAPTER LXVII.

FURTHER -E±PLANATIONS.

That was a tragic scene in the lonely dungeon— the rescued captive with eyes upraised to heaven, the lantern light falling upon his working features —the group around him overcome with joy, sur- prise—a hundred emotions all too great for utter- ance—Pietro writhing in the torture of baffled 'villainy, and the shadows and the black walls shut- ting them all in, like the frame to a picture. Miss Norrey's retreated unseen to the rear of the group. The butler, Morris, fell on his knees, sobbing "Lord Darkwood ! My dear, dear master !" he cried. "Oh, my dear lord, we all believed you dead ! -Oh, this is a happy day for Dunholm ? Welcome, we come, dear master f ' ale kissed the hand of the marquis, who trembled

like a leaf. "Lord Darkwood r cried Mr. Sutton, bounding forward. "Oh, mr dear lord--" Words failed him. Like Morris, he sobbed aloud. And Lord Darkwood stooped and folded his arms around him in an answering emotion, and a gladness that could find vent only in tears. Presently the marquis • recovered himself. He raised his head, and his gaze fell upon the writhing Pietro. "Where is his master ?" he asked.

- "Captain Tollish is in the library, my lord," claimed the butler. "I know a speedy route to the library. Come with me, all of you. Let us find the villain before he takes the alarm. But wait l I have a fellow prisoner— —" "Here she is, my lord," said the viscount. "We have just rescued her . The marquis drew near, and gazed upon the piteous little face, so tender and lovely in its pallor, and his countenance kindled with emotion. "We have been fellow sufferers young lady," he said gently. "Let us thank heaven who has delivered us, and these dear friends who have been sent to our-relief I—Bu4-oese.---,Let US find Captain Tollish I" They retraced theft.' steps to the upper portion of the . ruins, and the marquis conducted them along a passage, pausing at last before what seemed a blank wall? - He felt tremulously about its sur- face, and a spring clicked suddenly, and a door new inward, and the group stood in an aperture in the walls of the library. The room was lighted. Captain Tollish sat there with bowed head. He had not heard the secret door open, but now, as though some subtle instinct warned him of alien presence, he looked up and

beheld his rival. He glared at him as if slowly turning to stone, unable to credit the evidence of his senses.

Lord Darkwood stepped out, into the library.

Captain Tollish gave a great gasp, and pit his , hand to his forehead. "Fabian Tollisb," said the marquis sternly, "your day is over! I have suffered a long imprison- ment at your hands, but lam at last free! And new comes the hour of my vengeance !" Captain Tollish rose up, his fat face growing purple, his eyes protruding, his mouth moving in- audibly. Then he reeled and fell forward, stricken with a fatal apoplexy. Pietro gave one look at his master, and then wrenching himself from the bands that held him, he bounded to Captain Tollish's side and seized a pistol from the inner pocket of his master's coat. "He is dead !" said the Maltese. "The shock of finding Lord Daikwood alive has killed him He believed the marquis dead and buried. We carried his lordship below after the capitano sup- posed he le ' killed him and my master left me to bury him. it his lordship revived, and I thought to keep him captive and sell the secret some day to my master. My chance is gone. My game is up. , I have lost the marquis, the girl, and all. Yet one thing remains. My lord, will you give me free- dom—let me go unharmed—if I will give you Clare Mo.rkbam's child?" " What 1" cried the marquis, " the child is dead. Dog--" "Stay !" said Pietro. "Refuse, anal shall kill myself with thi,s pistol, and so balk you of any vengeance upon me. Promise, and I will give you Clare Markham's child !" "If the child lives, I promise." " The mother is dead," said Pietro. "But there is her child," and he pointed at Gwen. "Her child and yours, my lord I Miss Winter, I told you that I could restore you to your heritage. You are the Lady Gwendoline Charteris, the lawful daugh- ter and heiress of Lord Darkwood I" There was no lying in the voice and manner of the Maltese. The marquis turned to the girl, took her face between his bands, and kissed her solemnly, with a tenderness that went to her soul. "It was because she was your child that I pre- sented her," said the valet. "I meant to marry her and present her claims as your heiress and be- come master of Dunholm Castle. It was a lofty ambition, but it o'er-leaped itself ! Prove what I ; have said, my lord : find it truth. This girl is your own daughter I" "We will investigate Pietro's statement, my lord," cried Mr. Sutton.. "Pray heaven it may be proved true. We owe our present happiness to idiss Norreys. It is to her, my lord, you owe your rescue. Let me present you—" He looked around, but Miss Norreys had dis- appeared. When she had gone no one noticed. She had slipped out some minutes before with he faithful Aga, and was at that moment in the wag- gonette, speeding toward Beeehniont. Mr. Barsby hastened after her. The others remained at Dunholm Castle until morning. Gwen told the story of her life, and little doubt was entertained of the truth of Pietro's story.

AN IRISH MIRACLE.

INVESTIGATION BY A REPORTER.

From the "Tyrone Constitution," Aug. 25, 1893. Reports having constantly reached us of miraculous cures by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People—in many cases where the resources of the pharmacopceia and the orthodox prescriptions of the medical profession failed to bring relief to the sufferers—it was determined to investigate the first case conveniently near which we heard of. An opportunity presented itself last week which was taken advantage of. A workingman named Elliott, living in Colebrook, County Fermanagh, wrote stating that he bad been restored to health by the Dr. Williams' Pink Pills after having taken a largo quantity of medicine prescribed by' various members of the medical profession without the slightest benefit. An Interview took place on Tuesday last. Colebrook, where Elliott resided, is a wayside station on the Clogher Valley Tramway, and one of the pretti- est spots we have ever visited. But our mission this morning was to investigate a case in which it was asserted that a nineteenth century miracle had occurred. At the outset it is but fair to admit that we entered upon the investigation with but little belief. On arrival at the station we were informed that Elliott was at his work, but had left instructions that be was to be sent for on our arrival. He proved to be a youngish man, some- what below the middle height, spare and sinewy. At a glance we saw that he had met with an accident earlier in life which disfigured his face, and also that his right eye was obscured by cataract. In answer tea query he said it was his belief that his illness and suffering had their origin in the accident referred to, 'and he was requested to commence the narrative at this point. The following is a summary of his story, embodying all the facts About five years ago a horse lashed out and struck him on the face. pis nose was split and flattened and his jaw broken. His injuries were so serious that the doctor called in to attend him advised his removal to the Enniskillen Infirmary, where he lay for nearly two months. Shortly after his admission he recognised that he had lost the sight of his right eye. The shock and injuries combined broke down his constitution, and a permanent weakness seemed to have seized upon him. He was scarcely able to crawl about, at the end of two months.

I The poor fellow returned home weak and miserable. Time brought but little improvement. His strength seemed to have permanently deserted him. Able to crawl about., but no more—such was his condition for three years after the accident. Then a little strength returned, and with it hope. He /began to do little light work, and did not feel so utterly dependent. His full measure of strength might yet return. But about twelve months ago while at his work he was attacked by a new ailment—an excruciating pain in his side. It passed away, and he continutd work for two or three days. Another attack followed which utterly prostrated him. The doctor 'told him he was suffering from indigestion, nervousness, and palpitation of the heart. Medicine was prescribed, but without giv- ing relief. His old weakness returned, new complicated by excruciating pain intermittent in character, and changing from side to side. Prescription after prescription was tried but no improvement came. "In fact," said he" some of the medicine had the effect of making the attacks more frequent and the pain more agonising. Many and many a time so intense was my suffering that my screams could be heard ou the road" (a couple of hundred yards away). Medical skill could do no more, and the sufferer found himself doomed to a life of misery. _ . _

" Somewhat over two menths ago," said he, "I read in the papers of marvellous cures effected by Dr. Wil- liams' Pink Pills for Pale People, and although I had very little lope that they would be, useful in my case I sent for a box and tried them. When I had taken four cr five a marked improvement set in. The attacks became less frequent, and of shorter duration. The pain was not nearly so agonising. This gave me hope, but at times I dreaded that the improvement was only transitory, and that I would relapse into my ,old hope. less, miserable condition. I kept on ,taking the pills. Strength began to come, and with it a cessation both of the attacks and the pain. When I had finished the first box there was neither pain nor ache. I have now taken three boxes, and I feel better and stronger to-day than I have been for the past three years. lam still taking Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. I believe they not only culled my disease, whatever it was, but they are in some wonderful way building up my previously shattered con. stitution and giving me strength. lam very hopeful of the future. The effect of the Pills in my case was really miraculous. Ido not know how to express my gratitude—deep heartfelt gratitude." This case fully verified; as above shown by first-hand evidence, confirms the evidence which is accumulating on every hand, to show that Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People are one of the greatest modern medicines—a perfect blood builder and, nerve restorer, curing such diseases as rheumatism, neuralgia, partial paralysis, locomotor ataxy, St. Vitus' dance, nervous headache nervous prostration, and the tired feeling resulting there- from, diseases depending upon humours in the blood, such as scrofula, chronic erysipelas, &c. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are not a purgative medicine. They restore pale and sallow complexions to the glow of health, and are a specific for all the troubles peculiar to the female sex, while in the case of men they effect a radical cure in all eases arising from mental worry, over-work or ex- cesses of whatever nature. CHORAL COMPETITIONS IN MAN- CHESTER.

SUCCESS OF THE RUNCORN TONIC SOL-FA CHOIR.

- The second annual tonic sol-fa festival under the auspices of the Alatichester Tonic Sol-fa Council was held on Saturday, at the Botanical Gardens, Old Trafford. In the afternoon there was a choral com- petition for schools, for which nine entered. Each choir sang the part song "Thee we hail, 0 forest fair," and also. a piece of music composed as a sight test by the adjudicator, Mr. W. Decksey, music superintendent of the Bradford School Board. In the evening there was an adult choral competition. The following were the competitors :—Saiem Choral Society, Mossley Vocal Society, Bowdon Glee Society, Farnworth Choral Asso- ciatico. Runcorn Tonic Sol-fa Choir, Stretford Tonic Sol- fa Choral Society., Bredbury Choral Society, and Bolton Vocal Union. Each choir had to sing Thomas Morley's madrigal, "Fire, fire, my heart ;" a piece of its own selection, and a sight test composed for the occasion by Mr. F. Boyle. The adjudicators were Mr. W. Docksey- and Mr. R. H. Wilson, chorus director to Sir Charles Halle. The competition took place in the coneert room and was followed with close interest by an audience which crowded the hall. The first prize was awarded to the Bredbury Choral Society (conductor. Mr.J. Brierley) the second prize to the Runcorn Tonic Sol-fa Choir (con- ductor, Mr. J. Holford), and the third to the Messley Vocal Society (conductor, Mr. John Shaw). • The prizes were distributed by the Lord Mayor of Manchester (Mr. Alderman Marshall), who, in present- ing the medal to Mr. J. Holford, of Runcorn, said it afforded him great pleasure to do so, especially as this was the first time his choir had entered for competition. (Applause.) He hoped it would encourage them to pur- sue their work with renewed zeal and energy. The suc- cess of the Runcorn Choir gave great pleasure to the members and their numerous friends and supporters. When it is taken into consideration that the Runcorn Tonic Sol-fa Choir had only a month for preparation,and that they were new to the isiork of competitions, it must be freely admitted that they have done remark- ably well, especially when it is considered that of the otherieven choirs who had taken part in the competition, five had entered the lists on previous occasions, and of them there or four had secured awards. The medal obtained by the conductor of the Runcorn Tonic Sol-fa Choir, is of silver with gold centre, elaborately embel- lished' by the scale used in this musical method. It is exhibited in the Window of Mr. Coventry, High-st.

'

-D-EMONSTRATION AGAINST WORKMEN AT HAY- DOCK. _ MR. WOODS'S WARNING.

During the past fortnig,ht the chief topic of conversa- tion amongst the Haydock miners has been alleged clandestine coal-getting by " knobsticks "at Messrs. Richard Evans and Co.'s Pits. Complaints were made to Mr. S. Woods, M.P., miners agent for the district, the outcome of which was the publication of a letter last week warning the men of the consequences if they did ! not stop getting coal. This, in the opinion of the strikers I has had no effect, and on Wednesday a body a men, numbering about 100, visited the No. 3 Pewfall pit to meet the men as they left off work. The men all belonged to Haydock, a large number having been employed before the dispute at the pit in question. So quiet was the affair kept among them that the police did not get " wind" of it until after the possession bad started from the Huntsman Inn. Information was thea conveyed to the police headquarters, and twenty of the extra constables stationed at Haydock, under Inspector Pickering, went to the pit in a railway brake-van. They arrived there about ten minutes after the strikers, and their appearance packed in the van and on the engine caused a good deal of amusement. The majority of the men were armed with sticks, those who had not started with them stripping the trees along the lam to the col- liery. Although on arrival Inspector Pickering ironically remarked that the mob looked very formidable, their tone denoted that they were determined to carry out their mission. The men waited about the colliery for about an hour, during which time Mr. Fairhurst, the I certificated manager, told them that only officials were I working, and tkey only to keep the pit in order. At four o'clock seven men, presumably firemen, were wound up the pit, and the strikers asked them why , they had been working, and warned them that they would come the night following with a larger gang if they did not stop. The workers, who live in close prox- imity to the pit, were escorted home by Inspector Pick- ering and staff, Mr. Barrett, shareholder, Mr. George, secretary for the company, and Mr. Fairhurst being also present. Oc the way home the workers were hooted and Jeered at, bat no violence was used. Mr. Fairhurst sub- sequently addressing the men, said most of them knew the dangerous nature of the pit and workings, and that if men were not employed to clear the roads the pit would soon make up, and they would not be able t return to work. A number of men said the company would not want them back again so long as they could get men to keep the pits clear. If no men whatever had worked the strike would not have lasted a week. The officials then returned to the pit, and the men quietly dispersed. It was feared that a more serious disturbance would take place on Thursday night, as it was riimoured that the colliers intended asking some of their comrades at Park-lane to come over and assist them. Up to the time ot writing, however, everything was quiet, aid the police bave had no necessity to leave their quarters. The men still maintain that coal is being wound up, the Pewfall pit, and the Wood pit, but we are authorised to state that no coal whatever has been obtained from either pits since the commencement of the strike. All that is being done is to keep the pits in repair, and at the Wood pit one of the air ways is being widened, which necessi- tates the employment Of several men below ground.

THE COAL TRADE CRISIS.

MINERS' MEETING AT IIAYDOCK.

A meeting of miners was held in the Football Field, Heyde3k, on Friday morning; last week, for the pur- pose of considering the qu,stion as to the offer of arbitration which has been made to the men. There were about 800 present. Mr. James Bailey, a miner, occupied the chair. Mr. Edmund Walker, of Bryn, district secretary, ad- dressed the gathering. After explaining the cause of strike to the effect that it was due to a 25 per cent., re- duction in the men's wages being demanded on the ground of decrease in trade, he contended that trade had not decreased in the slightest. Taking the output of coal in 1888—and that was previous to the advance they had got—the oetput at that time was 172 million tons, and during the year 1892, nearly five years after that, the output was 190 million tons, making an In- crease of no less than 20 million tons. In the "Colliery Guardian " it was stated that the export trade for the first four months in this year was 290,000 tons more than it was for the first four months of last year. That again went to prove that there was not a depression in the trade, as the employers wished them to think. The second claim on which the employers had based their application for a reduction in the wages was because other counties had had reductions in wages, and be- cause of that it was impossible for them to sell in the same market. They gave the men to understand that Ayrshire, in Scotlana, was one of the places where a re- duction had taken place,and they said it was impossible to sell in the Scotch markets with their competitors from Scotland. They said that Ayrshire miners had been reduced 2s. Well, they found to-day that in con- sequence of the present struggle,the wages of the Ayrshire miners had gone up 2s. In his opinion the object aimed at by the masters was not so much to reduce their wages as to break down the Federation. If they accomplished that object, it would mean that the men would be prac- tically slaves in their hands, and whatever they (the men) were asked to do, no matter how low the rate of wages, they would, if they had no Federation, have to perform it. It was, however, no use of their thinking for a single moment of trying to break down the Federa- tion, for it would be a hopeless task. (Hear, hear.) There was one particular party of whom he wished to say something, and that was their own employers, Messrs. Richard Evans and Co. They were no doubt aware that there were men workingat the present time at several of the pits under this firm, and it was alleged that some of these men were engaged as day wage men. They had power to work as day wage men to keep the mines in proper order. He (Mr. Walkden) contended that any man who was down the mine, whether be be a fireman or a day wage man, if he were honest to the principles of the Federation, he ought not to be there. (Hear, hear.) Speaking honestly, be thought they would not have been out on strike but for Evans' firm and two or three more in Lancashire. He expressed himself as opposed to arbitration, and cited cases where in his opinion it had resulted unjustly. Their own firm accerd- ing to the statements that were made to him every month, men had been going away from Messrs. lEvans' not one week, but many weeks, with less than 15s. to keep a wife and children upon. Were they asking to arbitrate as to whether that 15s. should be reduced to 13? If so they might as well ask them to drown themselves, and the latter course wculd be the better of the two. (Applause.) When the next County Council election came round let them remember what had been done on the present occasion in flaydock. He was surprised that such a thing had been tolerated. Extra police had been sent into the district, not to prevent them from rioting, but to try and force them to riot. They ought to protest against it.and find out who had caused them to be sent there. When asked for their votes they ought to say toi the candidates, "No, you sent us policemen when we did not require them, and we will not send you to the County Council when we do not require you."( Laughter and applause). Mr. Walkden then put several questions to the assem- bled colliers. The first was, " Will you agree to a 25 per cent, reduction in wages, or any part thereof ?" There was a unanimous reply in the negative. He next asked for a show of hands, first from those in favour of any reduction, secondly those in favour of one per cent. reduction, and thirdly those in favour of arbitration. In each case not a single hand was raised. Upon the ques- tion being put as to who was against arbitration, a per- fect forest of ham& went up, the proposal being re- jected unanimously. The gathering also expressed themselves unanimously in favour of all the men remaining out. The meeting then dispersed. SYMPATHETIC LETTER FROM A COLLIERY PROPRIETOR. •

On Monday, s mass meeting of colliers was held on Wrexham Racecourse, for the purpose of pledging the strikers to remain united in resisting the masters' terms. Mr. loan T. Williams, the agent for North Wales, said he had a letter to read to them from an employer of labour in North Wales, and a larger employer in North Stafford- shire. As some of them knew, the Messrs. Craig had taken Brynkinallt CJllery, Chirk, and one of their sons —Mr. Ernest Craig—wrote to his father to tell him about the distress in the district, and to announce the starting of a relict fund. Mr. Craig wrote in reply as follows "Milton House, Alsager. Cheshire, Sept. 7,1893. . "My dear Ernest,—l have your letter stating that a fund for the relief of the miners' families who are on strike in the district is being got up. I am glad that this is so, for whatever happens, and whatever may be the opinion of the cause of the strike, we must help our fellow men in their distress. I have always held that the employe's made a great mistake in asking for 25 per cent. reduction. Such a demand is unprecedented in the history of the trade, and, in my opinion, most un- reasonable. On the other hand, I think the men 'would have done better not to have applied the 20th rule, re- quiring all to stop work who had not received notice, and the produce would have kept the manufacturing works on, which is most important, and they would have been ready to consume the coal when production was generally resumed. Besides, the limited output would have raised prices to a higher scale, and thus assisted the men to make good their pasition. As it is, these 'con- suming works may not start when the pits start, and these works must either continue to stop or the coal must be banked. However, this has nothing to do with providing the hungry with food. In this all can join, and I only hope that no one is so brutal as to desire the men to be beaten tiv actual starvation. I enclose you cheque for £lO from me personally, and you can join £5 from the firm if you and Samuel think proper. It is very distressing to see intelligent and reasonable men, such sal met about a fortnight ago, debarred from earn- ing their living by a state of things which would never have arisen had the proposed reduction been within the bounds of reason.—Your affectionate father. W. CRAIG." Loud applause followed the reading of the letter. The resolution was then put to the meeting, and carried un- animously. INQUEST ON TUE DEAD MINERS.

The coroner's inquiry into the cause of the death of James Arthur Duggan, one of the miners who died from a bullet wound received during the conflict at the Featherstone collieries a week ago, was resumed aod concluded at Wakefield on Wednesday. The witnesses stated that though both before and after the reading of the Riot Act ample opportunity was giveu to the mob to disperse, they remained, and even after the soldiers had fired they continued destroying property until four o'clock in the iriorninc... The jury returned a verdict of justifiable homicide." The inquiry concerning the death of James Gibbs; the other miner who was shot, was resumed at Featherstone on Wednesday; and after hearing the evidence of a number of miners, who denied that there was any rioting or stone-throwing when the soldiers fired, the jury returned an open verdict. DURHAM COAL FOR ST. HELENS GAS WORKS. The Gas Committee of the St. Helens Corporation on Wednesday decided to give a Durham firm an order for 300 tons of coal for the St. Helens Gas WDrks, the price , quoted, including carriage from Durham to St. Helens, I being lower than the price quoted by coalowners in St. Helens or Wigan.

COAL GEITING AT ST. HELENS. SINGULAR SCENES. SELF-MADE COLLIERY PROPRIETORS.

The colliers in S. Helens, in their efforts to provide for their families, have hit upen a novel expedient—viz. the opening out and selling of surface seams of coal, and in several parts of the borough some extraordinary scenes are being witnessed. A correspondent on Thursday morning visited Island's Brow, 'in Merton-road, and there found operations proceeding on a large scale. On a por- tion of the Orrell estate, owned by the Rev. Fathm. Leitrim]' a large band of men were engaged. They had sunk holes to a depth of from six to twelve feet, and then had started the " face "of the coal under the bill. Some had timber to protect them, but others had none, and work was going on under circumstances of con- siderable danger. Again, others were going straight down, and as small portions of a coal seam were exposed it was picked out and thrown to the surface. Men with harrows and women and children with buckets were carying it away, and in some cases large heaps were piled up. The work was carried On during the whole of Wednesday night, and fires were made on the hill side to assist the work. It is s•iid that some forty or fifty tons of fairly good coal have been got, and on Thursday morning coal dealers were at the place with lurries ana bags making bargains for the stacks of coal. The men realised sd. or 6d. a bag, and by this means a good re- venue was coming in. Another busy scene of activity was witnessed on land behind the Hardshaw Brook Chemical Works, belonging to the United Alkali Company. Here colliers have sunk a dozen holes to a depth varying from ten to forty feet, and are getting out coal and selling it honseholders at 9d. and ls,the barrow load. The men at each hole have a mutual understanding as to the amount of work they shall do, and as to the division of the money which is brought in. Some of the difficulties of an ordinary mine are to be seen. Water has come in some holes, and the men are getting the coal out of the water. The coal is pulled up in buckets, for the men are short of headgear and have very little timber to safeguard them in their work. On Hardshaw Fields near the Town Hall, surface coal has been found, and digging operations were going on during last Weduesday night and again on Thursday morning. It is said that coal is close to the surface near Knobstick Hall in Sutton, and that it is being utillged by crilliers and their families. The greatest good humour prevails amongst these self elected coll4;ry owners, and many jests as t. the proprietorship are heard. One man on Hardshaw Fields being asked what he was looking for, replied "25 per cent." and was told he might timid it if he went low enough. Some were mked if they had I the consent of the federation to this coal getting, and on which they intimated that they would look after them- selves in this respect. THE MINERS FEDERATION MEETING. Very great interest was taken in the proceedings of the Miners' National Federation, which met at Nottingham on Thursday. The special business of the meeting was to receive the results of the ballot taken on the three questions-1, whether a reduction of wages shall be ac- cepted ; 2, whether the dispute shall be settled by arbi- tration ; and 3, whether men who can do so shall resume work at the old rate of wages. ' Mr. B. Pickard, M.P., president of the Federati.n, occupied the chair, and though all the districts were represented there were not so many delegates present as at the last Conference. The proceedings were conducted in private, but it is un- derstood that to the first two questions the men in every district have by overwhelming majorities replied in the negative. Interest now centres in the decision on the third. On this question no discussion took place on Thursday. The Contgrence passed a resolution of sym- pathy with the relatifes of the two men who were Miot by the soldiers at Featherstone last week, and, while strongly denouncing all who take part in rioting as the worst 'enemies of the miners cause, expressed the belief that the presence of imported police and military in such large numbers was a forcible incentive to rioting and disorder, and urged the Government to withdraw at once all newly imported soldiers and police from the various counties affected.

PECULIAR INCIDENT IN A REVISION

COURT. TWIN SISTERS IN A DILEMMA.

A somewhat singular Case came to light at the Black- burn Revision Court on Wednesday afternoon. Agnes and Martha Cottatn, twin sisters, applied for votes as joint tenants of a dwellirw-house. There had been an agreement between the party agents that one only should have a vote. The question therefore arose which was the elder. Agnes, who was in court, confessed she did not know. The Revising Barrister said he was in a lix, and the Liberal agent, while objecting, sympathised with the applicants, as he was one of twins himself. The Conservative agent maintained that the twins were each entitled to a vote, and in this view the Revising Barrister concurred.

THE LOW RENTAL OF FARMS.

EXTRAORDINARY AGREEMENTS IN ESSEX,

In consequence of the extretWif;;;;SsiOn in agricul- ture, an extraordinary number of sales of farming stock are advertised to take place in Essex•at Michaelmas this year, and after quarter day the number of tenantless farms will be considerably increased. In order to keep farmers on the land several landowners have offered great concessions in the matter of rents, and in a few instances tenants who had given notice to leave. at Michaelmas have consented to remain on at a nominal rent. In one such case at least, near Braintree,a farmer has renewed his tenancy of upwards of 200 acres for another year at the nominal rent of £1 and tithes.

GREAT FIRE AT ST. :HELENS.

ALLEO ED INCENDIARISM. EXTRAOR.DINARY SCENES.

The premises of Mes,rs. Pilkingron *Brothers, the wa:11-known gia:s manufacturers and colliery pro- nrietore, at St Helens, were the scene of a vast con-. liagration on Saturday night and Sunday morniug, and there is, unfortunately, some reason to suppose that the fire had not an accidental origin. The ex- tensive works of Messrs. Pilkington Brothers are divided into two portions, namely, the plate-glass or " top " works at. Cowley-hill, and the " bottom " or sheet-glass works, situated In G rove-st. and Watson-st., a few yards from Liverpool-road, one of the main thoroughfares in the centre of the town. It was at the last-named department that the fire occurred, and never in its history was St. Helens the scene of such a brilliant illumination. The first indication to the tovvnspeople that anything was amiss was a ruddy glare which erase over the centre of the town shortly before eleven o'clock, and, crowded as the streets were on the market night, thousands of persons flocked in the direction indicated by the rapidly increasing brightness of the skies. It was soon ascertained that the extensive sheds for the storage of packing straw at Messrs. Pilkington Bros.' "Bottom" works were on fire, and the neWs aroused great excite- ment and alarm throughout the town. These sheds were built on the bank of the St. Helens Canal near Bridge- water-st., and Borne were of wood and others of brick. They were absolutely packed with hundreds of tons of straw—gathered in by the firm during the last few months—and with the solitary exception of one stack the whole stock fell a prey to the flames. The sheds and stacks seemed but as matchwood when attacked by the fire, and in less than half an hour after the first tilarm was given the whole area of the packing stores was one mass of flames. Attempts to suppress the fire at its height were absolutely useless, and for the first four or five hours the full strength of the two brigades was directed to preventing the flames spreading to the sur- rounding valuable property. Fortunately, these exer- tions were rewarded with success; but for more than twelve hours hosepipes were kept continually at work pouring immense quantities of water on the burning and smouldering debris. The circumstances surrounding the discovery of the fire, combined with serious assaults on police Jfficers and deliberate obstruction of firemen in the execution of their duties, have given rise to generally accredited rumours that the outbreak, was not purely accidental. The sheds used for the storage of straw included a wooden building, 120 ft, in length, and 30ft high, run- ning parallel with the canal, and a similar building at each end, forming three sides of a square. Inside this square was a brick building, measuring about 10 yards each way, and severalother smaller brick sheds adjoined the wooden buildings outside the square. Shortly after half-past ten one of the children of Mr. James Shepherd watchman for Messrs. Todd, Brothers, whose cottage is in Watson-st., at the corner where Todd's premises join the strawvard of Messrs. Pilkington Brothers, went into the backyard, but immediately returned to the house, crying," Father, there's someone running up the yard." Either one or two men were seen running from the direction of Pilkington's strawyard, and on Mr. Shep- herd rushing to the door he saw one of the stacks of straw on fire. He ran to the spot and endeavoured to knock out the blaze with his hands, one of which was burned in the attempt. He then warned the watchman at Pilkington's works, and the alarm of fire was given. The works brigade was immediately called up, and information of the outbreak was telephoned to the St. Helens Corporation fire brigade station. Superintend- ent Rutherford and a staff of men with the steamer and hose reels at once proceeded to the spot, where they found that the flames had got a good hold, and the wooden buildings and stacks of straw being a mass of roaring and crackling flames. The steamer was run on to the canal bank, opposite to the burning buildings.but after ten minutes pumping here the suction pipe became chocked with mud and dirt from the bottom of the canal and the engine was removed to the canal bridge, where, after considerable delay, a much better supply of water was obtained. In the meantime nearly a dozen stand pipes were affixed to the mains in Bridgewater- street, Grove-street, Watson-street, and the canal bank, and streams of water were soon being poured on the property adjacent to the burning mass, which was now roaring like an enormous furnace, and illuminating the country for miles around. As the roofs of the straw sheds collapsed one after another the flames shot up- wards to a great height,and the heat given off was almost unbearable to those many yards away. Great alarm was felt respecting the safety of the expensive timber yard and works oh Mr. S. Beckett, joiner and builder, which were only divided from the burning premises by the canal. In the bight of the conflagration the wood there commenced to blister and smoke, but hose pipes were directed to the spot, and scores of men, regardless of the terrific heat, threw hundreds of buckets of water over the wood exposed to the flames. The exertions were maintained for over an hour, and all fear of the flames spreading in that direction was allayed. During the height of the fire the conduct of a large crowd of roughs was disgraceful in the extreme, and they very seriously interfered with the firemen and police officers, who throughout the night worked like heroes in battling with the flames, and some of their acts amounted to malicious lawlessness meriting seven punishment. Shortly after the arrival of the brigade from the Town Hall a length of hosepipe was affixed to a standaipe at the corner of Liverpool-road and Bridge- water-street. The turneock left the shop to convey a message to the engineers, and on his return fonnd that the hnsepipe had been deliberately cut with some sharp instrument, rendering the pipe useless and necessitating the laying of another length. From this point and un- til the arrival of a large staff of police officers, under Chief-Constable Wood and Inspector Steel, the firemen were seriously obstructed in the performance of their duties, They were hustled and pushed about, and when they succeeded in getting the suction pipe of the steam engine into the canal agang of the roughs deliber- ately dragged it from the water, and yelled, " Let it burn," and " Let it burn tiro-- lot down." Several policemen ran to assist the firemen, but they, too, were obstructed, and some of them as- saulted. Some exciting scenes followed. Thousands of persons crowded and crushed along the canal hank, and the police endeavoured in vain to restrain them. Many persons, including a couple of policemen, were pushed into the water, but fortunately they were all rescued. In the course of the cdsturbances stones and other missiles were thrown at the officers, and cries of " Throw them into the canal " were raised. Police-sergeant Sisson had Iris helmet damaged by the stone throwing ; Police-con- stable Bradley was hit on the eye with a stone, and other officers received blows from sticks. Constable Crockett was the unfortunate recipient of a serious black eye. A man named Robert Edwards, residing at 37, Napier-st., was assisting the police on the canal bank, when half a brick, thrown by some scoundrel, struck Min on the Bide of the head, knocking him into the canal. Police- constable Carmody was standing by his side at the time, and the officer bravely entered the water and rescued Edwards, whose head was badly cut and bleeding pro- fusely. Some order was ultimately secured, and subse- quently operations were allowed to go on without interruption. 'rho destruction of the sheds and the blazing of the straw seemed to give intense delight to the rowdy element in the crowd, who lustily cheered at each fresh outburst, and audibly expressed hopes that the whole lot of the works would be destroyed.

APPLETON WESLEYAN CHAPEL.

On Tht;;;Sa—y—ali;ra;;;;;;;i:-; meeting, which was well attended, was held in the Wesleyan Chapel, Apple- ton, when a welcome was extended to the newly. appointed Wesleyan ministers of the Warrington circuit, the Revs. Owen Watkins and G. D. Mason. In the even- ing, the annual service in celebration of the inga thering of the harvest was held, the interior of the chapel having been, for the first time in the history of the building, decorated with cereals and fruit and flowers. Councillor Shaw Green, of Warrington presided, and interesting addresses were delivered by him and by the Revs. 0. Watkins, and G. D. Mason and by Mr, James Summersgill and others. A suitable selection of music was rendered by the choir,apd the proceedings passed off in a very enjoyable manner. PADGATE CHURCII. . _ -

Millard, M.A., lecturer at the Warrington Training College, and in the evening the Rev. H. Gibson Smith, rector of Halewood. The offer- tories were in aid of the Choir Food. PRESTON BROOK PRIMITIVE METHODIST CHAPEL.. . .

On Sunday, the harvest thanksgiving services were held in the Primitive Methodist Chapel, when two ser- mons were preached by a student from the Primitive Methodist College, Manchester. to large congregations. The afternoon service commenced at half-past two, and the evening at six o'clock, at both at which suitable anthems and hymns were rendered by the choir. The services were continued on Monday evening, when a miscellaneous entertainment was given, and arm address by the Rev. Win. Albert, of Runcorn, Mr. J. Whitley, of Stockton Heath, presiding. The chapel was neatly decorated with fruit, flowers, and corn, and having in the early part of the year been beautified, time edifice presented a presented a pretty appearance. Tho whole of the services were of a successful character. DARESBURY CIIURCII.
