RUNCORN PORT SANITARY AUTHORITY

At the monthly meeting of Widnes Town Czarricil on Tuesday, the Mayor (Alderman Gossage) presid- ing, the Health Committee reported, with reference to the Port Sanitary Authority of Runcorn, that the Town Clerk produced and explained drafts of two Orders proposed to be made by the Local Government Board—one forming a Joint Board, consisting of 13 members, being representatives of the Corporations of Widnes and Warrington, and the Runcorn Improvement Commissioners, and the Runcorn Rural Sanitary Authority, and constituting the same the Port Sanitary Authority tor the Port of Runcorn ; and also providing for, amongst other things, the number of representatives of and the contribution to the expenses of the proposed Port Sanitary Authority by each of the above authori- ties ; and the other authorising the Medical Officer of Health of the Liverpool Port Sanitary Authority to in- spect ships bound for the port of Runcorn, and also pro- viding for the isolation of persons suffering from cholera found therein, and for repayment by the Runcorn Authority of the expenses incurred by the Liverpool Authority ; toeether with letter from the Local Govern- ment Board asking for the observation of the Corpora- tion thereon.—The Committee resolved that the Council d-o adhere to the resolution of the 11th February, 1893, confirming and adopting resolution of this Committee of the 27th January, 1893, and do oppose the making of order constituting the Port Sanitary Authority, so far as the same proposes to place any part of the Borough of Widnes under the jurisdiction of such Authority. The General Purposes Committee referred to the sub- ject in the following terms r—The Town Clerk produced and read resolution of the Health Committee passed on the 29th ult., and the Committee resolved, that the Town Clerk be and is hereby instructed to further sub- mit the views of the Council to the Local Government Board, and that failing satisfactory arrangement,; being main to with that body, the Corporation do by notice in writing object to the order and oppbse the confirmation thereof by parliament, and that for such purpose the Town Clerk be and is hereby instructed and authorised to employ and pay Parliamentary agents, counsel and witnesses and do all such other Acts and things a; may be necessary and proper to be done in the matter. Councillor Neil,ehairman of the Health Committee,said some of the committee had considered the matter very seriously, and as they did not want to put the town to any unnecessary expense in connection with this author- ity, they had referred the subject to the General Purposes Committee to oppose it in Parliament if necessary. (Hear, hear.)—The resolutions were carried without further comment.

ALLEGED INSULT TO MR. GLADSTONE.

The BlitirgoVorie correspondent of the Central News telegraplat :—Mr. Gladstone attended morning service in the Episcopal Church at Blairgowrie on Sunday morning. The officiating clergyman, the Rev. F. W. Davis, in the course of his sermon made a somewhat uniNual and as it was though pointed allusion to the conduct of men who being servants of Mammon, strove only for worldly place and power. These words were taken to constitute an insulting allusion to Mr. Gladstone, and during the day they were excitedly discussed by the local Liberals. Mr. Davis, however, in an interview with the Central News representative, disclaimed any intention of referring " directly " to the Premier, but the rev. gentleman added that he was so bitterly op- posed to Mr. Gladstone's policy that on Sunday he re- fused to shake hands with him, and that if the Premier should call at the rectory he would not be received. The Press Association's Blairgowrie correspondent sends the following account of the occurrence :—When Mr. Gladstone presented himself at the Episcopal Church there on Sunday morning, and offered to shake hands with the Rev. Frederick W illiam Davis, the rector, the latter declined, and those who had hoped to hear the Premier read the lessons were thereforedisappointed. Mr. Gladstone occupied a seat near the front of the pul- pit. Some sensation was caused by several passages in Mr. Davis's sermon, which were interpreted as having special reference to the Premier. Mr. Davis took as his text the words" No man can serve two masters." He said many men tried to serve God and mammon, the latter being the favourite. How many came to the Lord's house that morning with a single purpose in view ? How many had been impelled by a contemptible spirit of curiosity only to set their laggard feet in God's house that morning. Did they think that God, who read the thoughts of all men, would not write down in the book of His rem'ernbrances that on a certain day in Septem- ber, 3.893, certain persons were prompted by most un- worthy motives to go into His house, and who when there only dishonoured Him by never thinking of Him ? Did they think that he would fail to record the fact that their coining into his House that day proved them to be hypocrites of the deepest dye, men and women who worshipped more Gods than one, who worshipped the creatures as well as or more than the creator. He asked them when that suicidal policy was to end, anel concluded by saying— Perish policy and cunning, Perish all that hate the light Whetlizr winning whether losing, Trust in God and do the right.

Mr. Davis on being interviewed after by a reporter did not conceal his dislike for Mr. Gladstene as a politician, but denied that any part of his sermon was intended specially to apply to Mr. Gladstone, and said had be desired to point remarks at the Premier he should not do so in church.

The Press Association states that Mr. Gladstone has written to a gentleman of Blairgowrie stating that he never offered to shake hands with the Rev. Mr. Davies, Episcopal Church minister of Blairgowie, on Sunday, and that the idta of doing so in church never entered his mind. .

RHYMES FOR THE PEOPLE.

AN APPEAL TO WORKING-MEN. 0 would ye be healthy, sober and wise, Be early to bed, and early to rise, And eschew the Publican's den; For his hours are long, His liquors are strong, And often prove fatal to men. Your work is severe in forge and in mill, And needs all your strength as well as your skill ; Then hurt not these blessings of God, In drinks that trepan, And sorely unman, The victims that trust iu their fraud. How small is your wage compar'd with your toil, In mill or in forge, cr delving the soil ; That idlers in grandeur may ride, Then waste not your cash, In Publican's trash, But remember your own fireside. And if you desire your cottage to see, Cheerful and bright as it always should be, Then keep out the Beer and the Gin : For where these appear, 'Tis certain and clear, That discord and sorrow come in. Your boys and your girls, all r.lastic as clay, Will follow your steps in chooslng their way; Be yours then to lead them aright, That they may detest The drinkseller's pest, And shun it by day and by night. If for your neighbour ye neighbourly feel, And always desire to further his weal, Then try to protect hien from drink; One glass might involve A breach of resolve And lead him in ruin to sink. Would ye put riot and drunkenness down That order and peace may reign in the town, 0 veto the Publican's trade; For surely euougli It is by his stuff That most of these evils are made. If Sunday ye prize, that calm day of rest, lieav'n's boon for the poor, and labour—opprest, . _ . .

Then seal up the Gin Palace door; - That drunkards insane, With tumult profane, May break on its quiet no more. - And surety ye know that Drink is to blame For most of our ills, and most of our shame, Against it, 0 then, take your stand; And work night and day To drive it away No more to dishonour the land.

FATAL FIGHT BETWEEN NEWSBOYS.

On Tuesday a shocking affair took place in a little', street running off one of the main thoroughfares in Bir- mingham. Two newsboys named Hands and Smith, ! quarrelled about their earnings and began to fight. Hands, who is 18 years old, was heated by Smith, who was only 15, and in his rage pulled out a penknife and struck Smith in the abdomen. Smith walked a few yards and then fell to the ground. He was aken to the hospital, but died on being carried into the building.

Farmers in East Essex have given their labourers notice that next week, audio some cases the week after, their wages will be reduced two shillings per week. In seme parts 12s. has been paid, and in other districts 11s. Great distress is apprehended In the rural districts during the coming winter. Already there are many labourers out of employment.

IT IS NOT A DYE.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

FRIDAY.

The division on the Home Rule Bill took place in the House of Lords early on Saturday morning. We are told that the scene was singularly imposing. The Opposition benches were crowded, as were also the cross benches and the seats in the neighbourhood of the woolsack. The bishops filled three benches in the portion of the house devoted to them, and even the Ministerial benches weie well sprinkled with peers. The space in front of the thrown was filled by privileged visitors, the galleries all round the house were fully occupied by ladies in elegant toilets, and many of them in full evening C 03.- turns. Many of the diplomatic corps amongst whom the Turkish Ambassador in a red fez was conspicuous, were seated in the same gallery. Taking it altogether, it may be . questioned whether a more striking coup d'iril was ever presented by the house on the occasion of an important division. The process of clearing the house was very slow owing to the crowding of the gangways by the large number of the Opposition who laird to make their way out. Several minutes were in fact thus occupied. The return of the peers to the house was of course more rapid. At length the division was completed, having occupied more than half an hour, and the leading Opposition tellers (the Earl of Limerick) proceeded to hand the paper conteirk- ine the numbers to the Lord Chancellor. .

The House of Commons held a sitting on Saturday,and went into Committee of supply. In the di.cussion of the vote for the Board of Agriculture Mr. F-1. Gardner said the Royal Commission on Agricultural Depression would boa non-party one. Neither birnetalism nor pre. ferential duties would he excluded from discussion. The administration of the department of the Charity Commissioners and the system of examination for en- trance into the public service were amongst the other subjects discussed.

MONDAY. In the. House of Commons on Monday, in reply to question, Mr. ARNOLD Mostar said a postal order tor ten shillings sent by Mrs. Wotherspoon, a pour widow, to her daughter at Blackpool had been stolen, and a care— taker in the service of the Post-office had been charged - with the theft. The Post-office was by law exempt from liability; but it might be a question for later considera— tion whether, as an act of grace, it would not be possible to give the woman compensation. Mr. T. E. Ettis promised inquiry into a grievance .at Pennrynydd, Anglesey, where it was alleged that William Jones had been threatened with ejection or actually ejected from an almshouse, chiefly because he had neg- lected to attend the parish church, such attendance being a condition of the tenancy. Replying to questions respecling the disturbances in the mining districts, Mr. ASQUITH said he had sanctioned the despatch of 250 London police to Yorkshire. The Government had no power to put an end to the dispute, but, in common with the whole nation, they earnestly hoped that counsels of moderation would prevail. The cost of the disturbances would fall upon the ratepayers of the districts in which they occurred. _

The House then went into Committee of Supply. Mr. Ilawnnity moved the reduction of the War Office vote, alleging that two officials in connection with the depart- ment had in their search for a new explosive picked the brains of inventors for their own personal benefit. ME: CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN said these charges applied to the period before he took office, bat be asserted that Mr. Banbury in his "railing accusation" had done two loyal officials consideraole injustice, and that he had himself accepted cordite, finding that it answered all the put. poses of a smokeless powder. The amendment was se- lected without a division. A long discussion follewed on. the Duke ofConnaught%. apt:ointment to Aldershot. As a protest Mr. Delmar- moved the reduction of the Commander in Chief's salary. but on a division the amendment was rejected by a Government majority of 117.

TUESDAY.

In the House of Commons on Tuesday Mr. H. Fowrant (President of the Loral Government Board). replying to Mr. Lees Knowles with respect to the condition of the Ship Canal, said the Salford Corporation were in a most perplexing position. The area impounded by the Com- pany contained sewage that was most injutous to the health of Salford, and he suggested that the Corporation should take legal advice as to whether the 'Company were entitled to create the niiisanee. It was extremely probable that the Corporation would have to go to Parliament for further powers to deal with the nuisance. The Local Government Board would assist them in every possible way to cope with the difficulty which now con- fronted them in the midst o: a dense population. • Questions were asked as to the powers of pcor-law guardians to provide work for the destitute, and also with regard to the railway rates on the North Stafford line.

Mr. SI7AW-LEFETIM said the Queen had given her con- sent to the hoisting of the national fig,. at Westminster during the sitting of Parliament, and that the cost would he £25 a year. Inquiry wa.s promised with regard to the prohibition of the miners' meeting at Barnsley. The House went into Committee of Supply, and on the. volunteer vote Mr. CAMPBELL-RANNERMAN announced that Her Majesty .had sanctioned the bestowal of a decoration on volunteer no-commissioned officers for zeal and efficiency in the service.

WEDNESDAY. In the House of Commons on Wednesday a number of Scotch subjects were discussed. Arrattempt was made to condemn Mr. E,slemont, the chairman of the Scotch Fishery Board, for speaking at a political meeting, but it was explained that he had simply replied to a per- sonal attack. Several votes were agreed to, and prc- gress was reported. On the vote for the Local Government Board Mr. U. FOWLER complained that Sir John Gorst had endeavoured to make political capital nut «f Ids attitude with regard to the unemployed. He was as anxious ea anyone that occupation should be found for the destitute, but he I pointed out that it would to, unwise without the consent of the Government for a Minister to adopt a proposat that'would practically do away with the existing poor- law policy. Sir WILLIAIt HARCOURT, questioned with regard to mining royalties in Wales, said the Government acted OD the principle of encouraging industries.

_ THURSDAY. In the Flouse of Commons on Thursday, Sir E. Gary, replyill to a question, said so far as the Government were aware no proposals had been made to restrict Bri- tish trade in Siam. Lord DutTerin was fully alive to the importance of British commercial interests, and would receive instructions in case tlyey were threatened.—Mr. Fowiant remarked that the local authorities were mak- ing careful inspection of food, and particularly of frut and vegetables from abroad. The report with regard to the el o'eraic outbreak ill the country was satistac oy, ith the exception of the state of things at Ashbourne, which was serious, eight deaths having occurred. These persons all dranlcat thef same pump, and the water was, to say the least, suspi- cious.

HORRIBLE :OUTRAGE ON A LITTLE - GIRL.

- At New Brompton, sear Chatham, on Sunday, a girl of nine was sent tor supper beer, and was returning home when a man suddenly pounced upon and brutally absaulted her. The ruffian dragged her through a fence into a garden, subjected her to shocking illusage, and cut her throat. The child was left insensible, but on recovering consciousness she crawled to the fence, and was seen and conveyed home. The girl, whose name is_ weeks, described her assailant. The child's condition is critical.

'HIE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY. SEPTEMBER 16, 1893.

IT is now some seven or eight The Coal War. weeks since the cessation of work! in the coal districts began, yet we seem to be as far from a settlement of the dispute as ever. A ballot of the men who ard locked out has been taken this week, and so far as can be! judged from an almost unanimous vote,their deter- mination to resist the reduction that has been demanded of them has not been shaken in the slightest degree by the anxieties and sufferings of the last few weeks. On the part of the coalowners, or at all events on the part of those owners who have banded together to close their pits, we hear of absolutely no movement in the direction of com- promise or conciliation. During the week one colliery proprietor has spoken out pretty plainly in condemnation of the policy of the confederated mine-owners. This is Mr. W. CRAIG of Alsager, Cheshire, who is a large employer in Staffordshire and in North Wales. " I have," he writes," always held that the employers made a great mistake in asking for twenty-five per cent. reduction. Such a demand is unprecedented in the history of the trade, and in my 6,pirnon most unreasonable." That is the plainly expressed opinion of a man whose sympathies would nat'irally, under ordinary circumstances, be on the side of the employers. There are, it seems to us, indications on many sides that it, is the opinion which is being come to by an increasing proportion of those who are net directly or indirectly interested in a reduction of miners' wages. There has during the week been a statement by another coal owner which has not, so far as we have noticed, received the attention it deserved. The coal owner we refer to is Mr. CHAMBERS, of Rotherham, and the statement he made was to the effect that the reduction that was demanded was only equivalent to two shillings off twenty-eight. On being challenged as to the accuracy of this statement, Mr. CHAMBERS had to admit that he had made a mistake, and that the reduction demanded was equivalent to a good deal more than two shillings out of twenty- eight. The _significance of this incident is to be found in the fact that Mr. CHAMBERS is one of those colliery owners who are most urgent in demanding the reduction, and, if we are not mistaken, he is a leading member of the coal-owners' executive _committee. That such a mistake should be made by such a man is, as CARLYLE might have said, "significant of much." Can it be that even some of the coal-owners them- selves have not realised how large is the reduction they are demanding? In the earlier weeks of the struggle, they very effectually bamboozled the public as to the real meaning of the " twenty-five per cent." Can it he that in the first place they had bamboozled themselves F The little wisdom with which nations are governed was the theme of a famous statesman; it may be that in the future, the present coal war will be taken as an instance of how lightly and ignorantly disastrous industrial struggles are entered upon. Itis unquestionable that the situation that is being created by the lock-out is rapidly becoming unbearable. Almost every industry is dependent upon the supply of coal, and as time goes on the mass of unemployed who are only saved from star- vation by public or private charity, increases at an alarming rate. The resources of the districts which are affected by the stoppage are already taxed to the uttermost, and a prolonged continu- ance of the strife will almost necessitate Govern- mental interference. This being the case it would certainly be well for the coal-owners to pay heed to a few warning words which appeared in the "Daily News" the other day. "The National industries exist," says this influential Liberal newspaper "in the last resort for the nation. If those who hold the stewardship of them cannot save us from horrors like those of the past week, their privileges will surely pass into other hands. The last word has not been said on the public management of the collieries, or on industrial co- operation in that branch of labour among the workers themselves."

The House IT was not the unexpected that ofhap- Lards and pened on Friday night last week— Home Rule, or, rather, early on Saturday morning—when tho HO-use of Lords threw out the Home Rule Bill. That their lordships had screwed their courage up to the point of refusing the Bill a second reading was universally known beforehand. There was some curiousity shown as to the voting, but it cannot be said that there was much surprise at the largeness of the majo- rity against the Bill, the voting being forty-one for, and four hundred and nineteen against. Everybody knows that when the unrepresentative Chamber ventures upon rejecting a measure of reform it does it with all its heart. And, besides, on this occasion there had been a special " whip- up." Many peers voted who are so little inte- rested in the affairs of the country, and so negli- gent in the discharge of their duties, that they never took their seats this session till the debate on Home Rule began. The action of the House of Lords has, however,—as pointed eut in a mani- festo issued this week by the National Liberal Federation—an important bearing on the system.

of Parliamentary government. It brings us nearer the time when the question of the re-construction or the abolition of the House of Lords will be th 3 most pressing question of the day. The rejection of the Homo Rule Bill is one of those events which brings home to all friends of progress the disadvantages of entrusting the power of vetoing popular measures to a Chamber that is perman- ently Tory and reactionary. In spite of the vote of the Lords the political situation is full of en- couragement to all true Liberals. To further quote the stirring language of the manifesto re^ ferred to, "The Ministry is strong, its supporters in Parliament are loyal, the programme is sound- Our opponents have played their last stake, and if we proceed with courage and deterrninationyne only will the Irish question be settled, but a real era of reform will be entered upon for the dog" cracy of the United Kingdom."

HILTON WESLEYAN CIIORCH. On Sunday and Monday harvest thanksgiving services were conducted in the Wesleyan Chapel, Elalton. Tim Church had been very prettily decorated, and copious donations of vegetatles and fruit had been artistically arranged, the whole effect being very harmonious and pleasant. A service of song was rendered in the after- noon by the choir, Mr. T. R. Frith, of Runcorn, giving the connective readings. The preachers Were the Rev. T. Wilkes, of Runcorn, and the Rev. J. Hanby, of Hal- ton, who, on the Monday evening expounded the signi ficanco of " A handful of corn." Laws Tassis.—G.LIILAND, Ironmonger, Warrington and Earlestown. THE WARRINGTON TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL. The monthly meeting of the above council was held on Tuesday, at the Horsemarket-at. Cocoa Rooms. A further appeal from the miners was taken into consideration, and several societies have remitted subscriptions to their relief fund. The bakers have sent £3; the wire drawers .£3; and a few painters and cleaners Ss. 61. The local file- smiths society have granted El per week for six weeks, should the strike continue. If prolonged beyond that period, to consider the question again of another grant. The prevailing distress in the town was di3cusBed to endeavour to bring about some means to alleviate those who are affected by the coal strike. One suggestion was, that the secretary commun;cAte with the chairmen of the different committees of the Corporation with a view to employing more men on public works, &c. The council being of the opinion that they are alleviat- ing the distress in that direction, but consider some- thing further might be done to assist those in immediate need. TRAVELLING TRITNKS.—J. S. GARLAND, 51, Bridge-st., Warrington. STRANGE SUICIDE AT SANKEY BRIDGES. On Sunday morning, a person named John Wil- liamson was walking along the bank of the Sankey and St. Helens Canal, in Sankey Bridges when he observed a man's hat nailed to a stump on the tow- ing path. This caused him to look into the -canal' when he saw a man in the water about four yards from the bank, with his face downwards. Mr. Wil- liamson got assistance and the body was pulled out and taken to the Black Horse Inh. The man was quite dead at the time. On inquiries being made it was found that the deceased was Joseph Eccles, painter, of 11, Ernest-street, Warrington. The inquest was held by Mr. S. Brighouse, on Tuesday, at the Black Horse Inn. The evidence of the deceased's wife showed that deceased got up at six o'clock on Sunday morning, and told her that he was going "mushrooming." She never saw him again alive. He was 37 years of age. He had been drinking for fourteen weeks, and had done no work during that time. He had said on Friday that he would drown himself if be did not get better, and had been in a very nervous state for some time.— Mr. Williamson gave evidence as to the finding of the body, and the jury returned a verdict of "Suicide whilst temporarily insane."

CROFT HORTICULTURAL EXIIIMTION. The ninth annual exhibition in connection with the Croft Horticultural Society was held in the Board Schools, Croft, on Saturday, by permission of the trustees of the school and members of the School Board. The Rev. H. I. Wadeson, rector, was the president, Mr. James Shaw chairman of the committee, which consisted of Messrs. W. Fazaker- ley, E. Monks, T. Abbey. W. Sanderson, N. Taylor, jun., J. Worthington, James Green, and G. Hatton ; Mr. John Sanderson acted as hon. treasurer, and Mr. Cawthorne the hon. secretary for the year. There was a large number of entries. The quality of the plants exhibited was fair, whilst the cut flowers had been somewhat damaged with stormy weather at the end of last week, and the exhibits in this class were not as numerous as in previous years. The fruit and vegetable classes contained some ex- cellent specimens. The• judge for the best culti- vated and neatest cottage garden was Mr. R. Harri- son, gardener to Mr, T. J. Gillespie, of Newton; and for the exhibits Mr. Harrison and Mr. Jackson, gar- dener to Mr. Smith, of Brookside House, Newton, whose awards gave general satisfaction, Ths opening ceremony was performed by the presi- dent. A picnic promoted by the Warrington Habitation of Primrose League was held on Saturday afternoon, when about 450 persons proceeded to Frodsham. Voisey (hon. treasurer), and C. H. Wood (hon. secretary). Mr. Pierpoint having been thanked for his address, dancing was resumed, and the party reached Warrington shortly before nine o'clock.

I LIINCORN.

BY AN OCCASIONAL CORIIESPONDENT.

Typhoid fever has been epidemic in Widnes for some time past and the 11.alth .Committee of the Corporation have held an inquiry into the cause.' The result of their investigation leads them to the conclusion that though climatic conditions have aided in the generation of the disease, the in- sanitary state of numerous hack passages had' 'Played a great part in causing tlit outbreak. Brought actions should follow such a finding as this and the black spots made as white as possible The Infectious Diseases Hospital is at present crowded with persons suffering from the disease, and Alderman Williamson, brought forward several , serious charges he had heard with rtsftect to the managerns.nt ofthat institution. It is satisfactory to know that the Health Committee hare decided to make an investigation into the allegations which in the interests alike of the staff and of the patiehts ought to be sifted to the bottom.

The appeal for subscriptions in aid of the distress in Widnes has met with a gratifying response. To their credit be it said, the working men et present employed are contributing - about two-thirds of the weekly subscriptions and it is true as ever that the greatest helpers of the poor in adversity are the poor themselves. The relief is being effectively distrib.ited by the Committee in the shape of soup and hot hots, and-no family need he without ope good meal a day. There are also private gentlemen who are providing dinners daily for those in need.

From the proceedings at the meeting of Runcorn Burial Board, on Wednesaay, it would appear that the cemetery field is not available for the purpose ,of a recreation ground, to whicl. use the Board of , Commissioners desired to apply it. At best this ' could have been but a temporary expedient, and the question had better be fairly and squarely faced new. Of the need of such a place there can be no two opinionstand it is a question for the ratepayers to decide whether they are willing to bear the bur- den its acquirement would entail. Could the free- holders be induced to invest their rights in the Commissioners, the Common might be utilised with great advantage, and at small cost. An effort wa., made in this direction some years ago, but a majo- rity of the ,freeholders were not in favour of such a course. Perhaps a change of feeling has taken place, and no harm could come of canvassing these gentlemen once more.

No labour and no expeLse are being spared to make the coming season ot the Runcorn Musical Society of the most successful character. The popu- lar taste will be well catered for by the arrange- ment of two miscellaneous concerts, but the chief effort of the season will be expended in the tiro- duction of that magnificent creation of, liandel's " Samp,on," for which there will be a full band and chorus. The production of Mr. Crossley's cantata "Adele "at a later concert will also be an event of more than ordinary interest.

The Commissioners' election which was brought to a conclusion on Thursday last week, excited deeper and more lasting interest than any previous election of recent times. The RatepayerkAssocia- tion did good service by organising public meet- ings and inducing the candidates to give expres- sion to their ideas on public questions, and to in- dicate how they would ask when the moment for decision arrived. It is for the advantago of all that the people and their representatives should be brought face to face as often as possible for the calm and deliberate consideration ,of questions, whether affecting sanitary requirements finance, or water supply. Such a.course not only widens the knowledge of the people, but must act as an incen- tive to the representatives to be more active in ad- ministering the law, and in forwarding schemes for the material and intellectual progress of the town. The acquisition of the waterworks for in- stance, *ll conduce greatly to the former: the Technical Institute when we have it erected will aid in the latter object.

The result of the contest will be gratifying to the Board of Commissioners from the fact that the three retiring members who sought to return their seats were successful. This fact would appear to show that the ratepayers are not se mindful of past ser- vices as we are sometimes lei to suppose. Mr. Imison who had the honour of heading the pall, a few votes in front of his friend Mr. Taylor, is an old and trusted representative ; therefore Mr. Collier is the only untried man, and he may rest assured that his career will be closely watched. It is a laudable ambition in a young man to desire to serve his town, and that Mr. Collier may have a long and honourable public career before him is the wish of those who cast their rotes in his favour, and doubt- less of those who did not.

-- Those who failed have our commiseration, but the way to public honours is studded with defects, and as hope rises eternal in the human breast ; courage for another fight will return when election time again arrives. There is one great cause for congratulation in the fact that probably the next election will be held under the Ballot 'Act instead of the present antiquated. cumbersome, and alto- gether unsatisfactory mode.

Since the election there has been considerable talk in certain quarters that politics largely entered into the contest. Of course there are people who can see politics in the mast ordinary actions of life. Mr. Imison in returning thanks after the declara- tion of-the poll said " Let me say this so that there may be no possible misunderstanding, don't let anybody claim this as a political victory. I know right well I have had scores, nay hundreds of votes from my Liberal friends; and my greatest enemy will be the man who tries to make it out that this has been a Tory victory. The Liberals have recog- nised that in this contest politics have no part." This may be considered an effective reply to those who state the opposite view.

MARRIAGE OF MISS BROADHURST AND

MR. HAILWOOD AT RUNCORN.

A numerous audience assembled in Runcorn Parish Church on Tuesday afternoon to witness the marriage of Miss Martha Broadhurst, third daughter of the late Mr. William Broadhurst, High-street, Runcorn, and Mr. George Hailwood, son of Mr. William flailwood, `of Hailwood's Buildings, High- street, Runcorn. The Rev. Canon Maitland Wood, vicar, performed the ceremony. The bride was attired in an ivory Bengaline silk, trimmed with lace, and her head was adorned with a wreath of orange blossom,from which depended a flowing veil. In her hand she carried a bouquet of white tuber roses and lilies and maiden hair ferns. The bridesmaids were Miss Alice Broadhurst (sister) and Miss Lillie Henshaw (cousin), and these young ladies wore dresses of French grey Crepe de Chine, trimmed with white Bengaline, with large black velvet picture hats with white plumes. They carried bouquets of white marguerites and maiden hair ferns. The bride was given away by her brother, Mr. James Broadhurst, while Mr. Percy Ford acted as best man. The other groomsman was Mr. Thomas Ford. The bridal party and guests were conveyed to and from church in six carriages and pairs from the establishment of Messrs. Yarwood Bros. Subsequently luncheon was partaken of at the residence of Mr. Thomas Ford, of Weston, uncle of the bride. For the accommodation of the numerous guests a marquee had been erected in the orchard and 1 the catering was entrusted to Mr. Wood of Bold-street, Liverpool. Amongst those present were Mr. and Mrs. Thomas Ford, Mrs. Broadhurst, Mr. Broadhurst, Miss ' Alice Broadhurst, the Misses Hailwood. Mr. Percy and Mrs. Thomas Ford, Mrs. Cooper, Mr. and Mrs. Thomas Cooper (Buxton), Miss Cooper (New Mills), Mr. and Mrs. Hatch and family, Mr. and Mrs. Venables (Rostherne), Miss Venables (Bolton), Miss Jones (Bolton), Mr. and Mrs. Henshaw (Didsbury), Mrs. Henshaw (Lymm), Mr. Harry Henshaw, Mr. and Mrs. E. Imison, Mr..and Mrs. Bridgewater, Mr. and Mrs. Sill, Mrs. Kirkham (Weston), Mr. and Mrs. Kenwright, Messrs. Pennington, Miss Meen, Miss Collier and Miss Lillie Collier. The usual felicitations were accorded to the young married couple, who during the afternoon departed for North Wales.

---4 - PENKETM

---- ENTERTAINMENT.—On Monday evening last, Professor Wyman of Widnes, gave an entertainment in the Board Sshools, Sankey. The programme was composed of con- juring feats, ventriloquial oddities, and a most amusing sketch the " Shadowgraph." The tricks were capitally performed and the way the Professor worked his figures proved that he was a perfect master of his art. There was only a moderate attendance. Mr. C. H. Hope gave some pianoforte solos during the evening.

THE SHIP CANAL.

THE CANAL ANI7.) THE COAL STRIKE. The Manchester Ship Canal Company, which has been using, when in full work, 10,000 tons of coal per month, is now importing large quantities of Scotch coal. Three steamers discharged at Saltport last week, and other vessels are expected this week. Failing these supplies, the Company had arranged to have their requirements met from the German and Belgian coalfields.

HEALTH COMMITTEE.

The Health Committee reported that 12 pigs suffering from swine fever had been slaughtered, and the owners paid half the value amounting to Ll 5 ss.—Typhoid Fever —The Committee having considered the reports 'f the Medical Officer of Health and Inspector of Nuisances with respect to the prevalence of typhoid fever in the Borough are of opinion that, whilst the recent outbreak is pri- marily attributable to the peculiar climatic influences of the present year, nevertheless the uncompleted state of want of sewers in large numbers*, of new streets and passages in the Borough assist very greatly in develop- ing the causes giving rise to the disease, and the Com- mittee are further of opinion that if such streets and pas- sages were properly completed and sewered the liability to similsr outbreaks would in the future be materially lessened.—Jfedical Officer's report—The Medical Officer repotted that during the past month there were 105 births and 73 deaths, being at the rate of 42.0 and 292 per 1,000 respectively. Twenty-seven deaths occurred amongst children under one year, and 18 amongst chil- dren between one and five years, Zymotic diseases caused 22 deaths. Notifications has been received as follows :—Diphtheria 8, enteric fever 49, erysipilas 7, membranous croup 2, puerperal fever 2, scarlet feVer 3, total 71. Although, adds the doctor, the death rate Is still far above the Widnes average, it is slightly lower this month than in June and July. The number of deaths from zymotic diseases,especiallytyphoid fever is still large cases of typhoid fever notified cottinue to be numerous. As the disease is fairly evenly distributed all over the town it is probably chiefly caused' by atmospheric in- fluences, such as the decompositiosi products of animal and vegetable matter due to the high temperature cir- culating in the atmosphere. We have had eight cases of diphtheria during the month,and it is likely that,but fot the change in the weather, a serious outbreak might have occurred. I regret to have to state that one of the nurses at the Fever Hospital was attacked by typhoid fever,and unf ort u nately succumbed. I have frequently visited both hospitals, and as usual, have found the management and attendance excellent. The Medical Officer of Health not being present at the meeting,it was resolved that his absence be recorded upon the minutes of the cOmmittee. It was reported that there were now 25 persons in the hospital suffering from typhoid fever, and most of them were convalescenc.—Councillor U. I. Neil moved the confirmation of the minutes. The fever was abating slightly, and in a short time there would be a few beds to spare at the hospital. It would not,there- fore, he necessary to employ district nurses as had been contemplated.—Councillor Carney seconded.

STAFF.

Alderman Williamson said there were remarks made in the town with reference to the Fever Hospital which should be investigated by the Committee. There were several complaints by patients. who had been is the hospital suffering from typhoid fever or small-pox that they had been insufficiently fed, and that they had had to perform duties there which were not required of them according to the regulations. In one case a patient after being out of bed for two days and in i_nother after being out of bed three days were sent into the washhousc at ten o'clock at .night to mangle and fold clothes. He did not think that was a duty which should be imposed upon any patient. The other complaint was entirely outside the duties, and was the washing of sick patients. This was a matter which should h attended to by the nurses and not by anyone, who hacr just recovered from such a serious illness as typhoid fever. He should like to know whether the Committee would ihquire into the matter. It was not right that the nurses should lie under the charges if they were not guilty, and on the other hand, if the hospital was not carried on competently the sooner it was and the better.—Councillor Neil said he was much surprised at these charges, and should like to ask the names of those who were responsible for the calumny. He referred the Council to the Medi- cal Officer's report, in which that gentleman said he found the management and attendance excellent. He (Mr. Neil) visited the hospital very frequently and he would not submit to anything being carried on at the hospital such as Mr. Williamson mentioned. (Hear, hear.) In the first place he declined to believe without ' good authority, that patients had ever been engaged in the washhouse or in washing patients ; with reference to , being insufficiently fed, the patients were fed accord• ing to the doctor's orders, and he hoped the medical rs:en would see that the patients were fed reasonably. His experience was that the patients when they left the hospital looked much better than ever they had pre- previously done iu their lives, or probably would again. (Laughter.) Alderman Williamson said he was prepared to place the names of those who had given information befere the Committee when they were inclined to take the matter up.—The minutes were carried.

GAS AND WATER COMMITTEE:

The Engineer reported as follows :—Burst Water Main—During the past month have been eight burst mains, the most serious of which was the 16in, trunk main in Victoria-road which gave way on the 27th ult., causing a temporary stoppage of the supply to the whole of the houses and works in Widnes, south of Deacon- road. The repairs were completed and the water turned on by 6 a.m, on the 28th ult. It should be pointed out that, but for the fact of most of the works being partly or entirely stopped, the inconvenience to the public which arose from this tem- porary stoppage would have been much more serious than it was. Proposed new 14in. trunk water main—A plan is herewith submitted shewing, a new line of 14in. trunk main along Lowerhouse-lane, which would con- nect the 16m. in Bi rchfield-road to the 121 n. in Ditton- road. Such a main as this is really required to meet the constantly increasing demand for the Corporation water by the works. A further advantage would be that of having a double line of pipes into the heart of Widnes, so that in the event of a mishap to either the constant supply, which is absolutely necessary for the works and greatly to the advantage of all householders, might he made more secure. Coal Supply—Resolved that the Engineer obtain 500 tons of coal from Messrs. R. Evans and Co., at the price now stated.—Water Main—Resolved that the Water main from Deacon road to Lugsdale-road be doubled at an estimated expenditure at £125. The illuminating power of gas during the month had averaged 21.52 candles.— Councillor Bullough proposed the adoption of the minutes

ItUNCOItN SCHOOL BOARD.

The monthly meeting was held at the Town Hall on Tuesday evening. Mr. J. Pritchard presided, and there were present the Rev Canon Wood, Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, Dr. McDougall, Messrs. S. Taylor, T. Hand- ley, and G. Moss (clerk). THE EVENING SCHOOL. _ .

The School Management Committee recommended that the following teachers be appointed to the Greenway- road evening school: Mr. J. F. Moulton, Mr. W. Epps, and Mr. L. W. Peacock; that the caretaker be allowed 4s. per week as before for his extra tune in connection with the evening school; that a class for girls be com- menced at the evening school, Miss Kilburn to be the head teacher ; the classes to meet twice per week, the fee to be 3d. per week, or 2a. ad. quarterly paid in advance; and that the Board join the National Associa- tion of School Boards paying an annual subscription of 21s. —Dr. McDougall moved the confirmation of the minutes. He alluded to the new departure of the Board in having classes for girls. The Board had hitherto not thought it advisable to have them, but it appeared that on every occasion H M. Inspector visited the town lie urged the starting of such classes. That was due to the fact that the Education Department was constantly sending circulars to them on the subject. The difficult-v the members thought had existed was that their distria was an industrial centre, in which the girls were usefully employed by their parents. It, however, might be c;1. advantage to fully test the experiment, and have the classes established. If the minimum number of scholars presented themselves the classes could be started and if more then it would he 'satisfactory to the Board.—Mr. Handley seconded the proposition, which was carried.

THE PENNY BANKS.

The Clerk submit.t-e-d-ii;e-..ii;t annual report of the savings bank at the Board Schools for the year ending August 20th last. They showed that the deposits were boys, £65 lie. 7d. ; girls, £B3 19s. 10d. ; and infants, £2805. 7d. ; withdrawn : boys £39 Bs. 4d., girls £5O 9,, and infants £l9 Os. sd. ; leaving balances of :—Boys £26 3s 3d., girls £33 103. 10d., and infants £9 Os. 2d. The total No. of transactions was £9,227, and the accounts opened were £276. The average amount in bank per scholar was boys 5-4. 7d., girls ss. 3d, and infants 3s. The members discussed the subject and con- sidered that although the banks were used in a great measure for temporary purposes they had been instru- mental In the encoulagement of thrift. Ultimately it was decided that the banks should be continued for another year.

THE SCIENCE AND ART CLASSES.

Dr. McDougall moved that application be made to the Board of Commissioners for a grant of £2.30 for the science and art classes. The Committee had had before them the report of the Science and Art Department,and these reports were not nearly so satisfactory as they had been in the previous year. The results were Unsatisfactory in this respect that second passes had leen abolished, and that examinations of second class passes had been stiffened very considerably. This applied to their classes and to all classes in the country. On Thursday after- noon Last, the Chairman and himself attended a meeting

lin Manchester for the purpose of helping in formulating certain resolutions that had to be submitted too Mr. Acland, vice-presideut of. the Education Department, ' pressing him strongly to return the old method of ex- atnination minus the grant. The Runcorn classes had been subsidised to the extent of about £2OO by the County Council, and from this fact it would be obvious to everyone that this new style of examination was a move on the part of the Department to lessen the ex- penditure of that department by making the cost of science and art education more of a local charge. The grants on first class passes had been increased, but this did not by any means meet the losses which were so very considerable, that actually teachers who were teaching, on the strength of having the grant,discovered that they were out of pocket, and in addition that the school had been fined to a large amount. The sum the Board were asking for that night, was calculated to meet all the requirements of the Technical Committee for the year ending Sept. 1894.—Mr. Handley seconded the motion which was carried. There was no other business.

RITNCORN IIUkCAL SOCIETY.

THE COMING SEASON.

The Committee ot Runcorn Musical Society have arranged a most attractive programme for the coming winter, and we hope their enterprise will be rewarded with " bumper " houses. The first con- cert is fixed for October 30th and will be of a miscellaneous character. The artists will be Madame Fanny Moody, Miss Lillie Arovdy, and Mr. Charles Manners (formerly chief principal with Carl Rosa's Opera Coy.), solo pianoforte Mr. Reynolds, and solo violin, Mr. F. H. Crossley. On December 13th will be performed Flandel's great work" Sampson," by the combined societies of Warrington and Runcorn, Mr. F. H. Crossley being conductor of both societies The artistes engaged are Miss Thudichutn, Madame Seeker- shay,, Mr. Paxton Smith, and Mr. Bantock Pierpoint. The combined choruses will number over 200 voices, This performance will be given in the Dr ill Hall. The third concert is programmed for March 14th. Mr. Crossley's cantata "Adele," which was received with much favour on its production, will form the first part, and the second part will consist of miscellaneous items. The arrangements with artists for this concert are not vet completed. At both the first and last concerts the choir will sing glees and part songs.

BOWLING AT WARRINGTON.

THE FARMERS' ARMS, FADGATE.

Lovers of this enjoyable pastime have had an exceptional season in which to display their prowess with the ".wood,' and on Tliur-day last week a bowling main of considerable local interest was commenced on the tine green of the " Farmers Arms "at Padgate. This was brought about by the landlord (Mr. Joseph Roberts) announcing a main for tradesmen and amateurs, and offering an array of prizes vely seldom met with, consisting of a handsome marble timepiece, several marble clocks, plated tea and coffee services, cruet stands, plated teapots, carvers, knives and forks, Stn., Sm., the whole costing the respectable amount of £l6, which was augmented by several prizes by local tradesmen. As might be expected the entrances were numerous, totalling exactly one hundred, and in consequence a good portion of play had to be postponed until Saturday. Fortunately the we tther continued all that could be thsired and on the adjourned day the green was again the scene of considerable activity. Some fine bowling was witnessed on both occasions, the final heat being most exciting, both men engaged therein, John Swanton of Warrington, and Thomas Hudsmith of Plank- lane, keeping remarkable even, the game standing at 12 each and 13 being up. The last end came off in favour of Swanton, he consequently winning the first prize. DISTRESS IN WARRINGTON.

INCREASING DESTITUTION. MORE SOUP KITCHENS OPENED. , The distress in the town goes from bad to worse, and the prospect of an early settlement of the coal war in this district seems to be as far off as eve?. The outlook is indeed serious fqr Warrington, as each week of idleness on the part of the miners,. causes additions; to the already too large number of unemployed in the town. Mill after mill closes,. and day by day the distress becomes more acute. IThere are now a number of agencies at work in the town doing their utmost to cope with the distress by distributing food and finding work for a few of the unemployed, but it is difficult to keep page with the demand. Each morning during the week men, women tied children in most cases scantily clad, and showing signs of hunger wet e to be seen flocking to the soap kitchen, at the Drill Hall to receive their day's support. Additional relief stations have been opened is Ellesmere-5.,, and Haydock-et., and the Football club have been distributing tickets for relief each day. Mr. Percy Davies has also distributed a large quantity of vegetables. Nor is the effect of the coal strike confined to the working-classes alone. Shopkeepers and property-ewners are also suffering, many of the unemployed not having paid any rent for some weeks, nor are they likely to pay any till the end of the struggle. Work at the large mills in the town will soon be at a complete standstill. At Messrs. Monks, Hall, and Co.'s works, the whole of the men will be thrown out of employment uatil the settle- ment of the strike, after to-day (Saturday). At the Whitecross Wire Works all the forges are stopped, and only one of the mills is going. Messrs.Rylands Bros. have also found it necessary to husband their stock of coal, and are now only working three days each week. At the Cheshire Lines Wagon works it has been found necessary to adopt a similar course. In order to avoid a stoppage, which would be dins- trotis to many of the workpeople, a special arrange- ment was made at Cockhedge Mills on Thursday. After a consultation with the overlookers, Armitage took a ' vote of the workpewle on the question as to whether the mills should be closed entirely on Wednesday next till the close of • the strike; or whether the workpeople should stibo• scribe a portion of the extra price that will have to . be paid for coals to keep the mills going. It waa decided, 1,200 workpeople, to subscribe a penny out of every with only three or four dissentients out of shilling earned towards the en hauce4 price of :coal. This will amount to about twe-thirds of, the extra price to be paid fcr the fuel, and the remainder will be paid by the firm The resolution will take effect after Wednesday night next, when the stock of coal in band will be exhausted. Orders were given yesterday by the firm for another supply of coal at the extra price. Only two or three mills have been running during the past week at the Dallam and Bewsey Forges, and these have been kept working to execute urgent orders.

THE DISTRESS AT SANKEY BRIDGES. The IVesievans of Sankey Bridges, who, during the course of last week provided free teas for those thrown out of work in that district, owing to the coal strike, gave another tea on Saturday night. About 180 men were provided with tea, and after the tea was over, an entertainment was given. The room was crowded to excess. The chair was occupied by the Rev. J. Kirtlan, who was supported on the platform by the Rev. Owen Watkins and Mr. W. ,Brough.—The Chairman, in his opening remarks said that while sorry that a strike of colliers had taken place, he did not blame the masters or men, as he did not understand all the intricacies that existed between capital and labour, employer and em- ployed. It was his opinion, however, that as far as the colliers were concerned, that no collier ever got too much money for ' the work he did. (Applause.) The collier's work was the most utap'easant employment that a man could engage in. lie was very sorry, however, that strikes could not be avoided, as they caused such widespread suffering amongst the men, women, and children of this country. He had seen a great many strikes during the course of his lite, and had taken great interest in them, but he never remembered men being shot down before the present strike. He hoped for the sake of humanity that this present strike would speedily come to an end, and that the men would again have full work. It was a great pleasure to the friends at Sankey Bridges to do what they could for thein,but they could only touch the fringe of the trouble. They could not affora to give a regular supply from day to day. He deeply sympathised with the ,nen and their families,and he was in thorough sympathy with those at Sankey Bridges who had given a helping hand to those in dis- tress. It would be a shame ot any Christian church to neglect those who had by no fault of their own, been brought to their present low circumstances. The church should look after the suffering, and he was glad that those of Sankey Bridges had done what they could.—A. glee "It was a lover and his lass" was then given by Messrs. Miles, George, Entwistle, Wilkes, Bailey, Mrs. T. Brough, and Misses Cannon and Morrell, and were very well received.—Mr.Whittaker next gave some selections on the concertina, and afterwards imitations on the concertina of various birds, including larks, thrushes, cocks, &c., and also bells ringing, in such a realistic manlier, that as the Rev. Owen Watkins said it almost appeared that he had the birds in his pockets. Needless to say, he was encored, and gave an imitation of the church bells.—Mr. R. Entwistle followed with " The Ray of Biscay," the audience singing the latter part.— Rev. Owen Watkins next addressed the meeting. He said that he had for 17 years been travelling in the in- terior of Africa. Like Mr. Kirtlan, he did not profess to understand the merits of the dispute between masters and men in this case, but he might mention that about 13 years ago he was in the Transvaal, and at that time all the implements of farming were made in England, but now the majority of them were made in America. Railways had been made there, but the rails and engines did not come from England, they came from Belgium. He mentioned these things to show that every strike which took place, drove away the markets from Eng- land, and opened the way for other people to come in and undersell us. A little while ago there was a lock out in Manchester, Oldham, Rochdale, and the district, which lasted 15 weeks. Me cost in wages that would have been earned and money that would have been made, was a million and a quarter, money which was thrown away,as much as if it had been carried to the sea and cast overboard. But that was not all The Lanca- shire people were only now beginning to realise that the continental spinners had been trying for years to estab- lish a cotton depot in Manchester, but they had always failed until this last strike. Since the strike, le. wever, it was possible to buy cotton of continental makes at the Manchester Exchange, of the same quality, cheaper than it could be bought at the factory doors in Lanca- sh;re. It was, therefore,of the greatest importance that before a strike was decided upon, each side should care- fully consider every point, and not do anything in a hurry. He thought that these bad times did some good however, and showed who were the workingmen's rest friends. He would like to know how much the publi- cans had done for them. Very little, he thought, and he hoped that when prosperous times came, they wourd remember it. Mr. Watkins concluded with an evange- listic address,and was very heartily applauded when he sat do w n.—A recitation was very effectively given by Miss A. Morrell, entitled' " The Road to Heaven." Mr. l'rincep sang "Friar of Orders Grey," and on being en- cored, sang "The Wolf." Mr. A. B. Bailey gave " Massa's in the cold, cold ground," for which he was encored but declined to re-appear.—Mr. 11. Morgan next made a very earnest speech, after which Mr. Wilkinson gave "The fire in the grate," for which he was encored. —Mr. Whittaker then gave selections from " The Bohe- mian Girl," which he executed very well, and on being encored gave "The song that reached my heart." A selection was then given by the string band in connec- tion with the chapel, and tho entertainment was con- cluded by some impromptu verses on the subject of the meeting by Mr. C. Pritchard. After the entertainment loaveewere given to each of the men to tide Sunday over. On Sunday the services at the chapel were very well attended, the evening service especially being extremely well attended, and the preacher' Mr. Collier was listened to with marked attention. On Monday night 350 chil- dren were fed, and on Tuesday night 160 women sat down to tea, after which a preaching service was held in the chapel by the Rev. J. Kirtlan for which the greater number remained behind. Wednesday was a clear day but on Thursday a tea was provided for 430 children who had their tea in the fielil behind the schools. Fri- day again was a free day, but to-night another tea will be given to the men out of work. A few particulars as to the wcrkers and the funds and expenditure will not be out of place here. A committee composed of the following gentlemen: Messrs. W. Brough„ S. Blythe, C. E. Bromley, R. Highley, A. B. Bailey, C. George, R. Entwistle, T. Woolley, W. Miles, J. Corra- dine, C. Pritchard and W. Bennett?, have the charge of all arrangements such as fulancial matters, the pro- gramme of the evenings, &c. The collectors who solicit subscriptiens are in pairs and are as follows: Messrs. C. E. Bromley and Baxter, Messrs. J. Cerradine and C. Pritchard, and Messrs. W. Miles and Woolley. Since the commencement of the relief on Monday week. £3l- - 41. has been collected, and the expenditure has been £22 3s. 91. thus leaving a balance of £9 14s. 53d. for the forthcoming week. Added to this Mr. W. Ben- nett of Padgate, has given twelve hamper 4 of potatoes to be given away to-night, to last the Sunday over. Other farmers round about have also presented potatoes. The t3tal number of those relieved up to the present has been 1,960 which have been fed at the cost of about 2d. per head. The provisions, to., used, include 300 four lb. loaves, 250 half pounds of tea, and 226 ounces of tea which have also The success in this beyond been givena n work has beenticiapwataiyon. , and a great amount of suffering has been prevented by the timely help that has been started none too soon for the poor of Sankey Bridges. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATE.

RETURNING WITH HIS GAME. Alfred France, 373, Winwick-road, was charged at the Borough Court on Monday,for having in his possession a net, and a quantity of rabbits, on the 25th August, sup- posed to have been unlawfully obtained. Defendant who was represented by Mr. Unsworth pleaded not guilty. P.C. Bell said he was in company with Con- stable Newey on the 25th ult, in plain clothes. When in I Winwick-road he saw the defendant coming from the neighbourhood of the Gas Works. He was carrying a net on his shoulders,and also a stick and a bag with nine rabbits in. On seeing them the defendant commenced running and dropped the bag and stick. P.C. Newey gave chase, and the defendant threw his net down. P.C. Newey corroborated, and said when the defendant tbrew his net down he (witness) told him he had no cause to run as that was all he wanted. Mr. Unsworth con- tended that it was a case of mistaken identity and that the summons had been left at the wrong house. De- fendant was fined 10s. A LODGING-HOUSE KEEPER'S LICENSE CANCELLED.

At the Warrington Borough Court, on Monday. Jeremiah Shorrocks was charged with having on thesth September, in Ship-yard, unlawfully suffered four lod- gers to occupy beds within eight hours after they had been vacated by the previous occupants. Inspector Powell said on the sth inst. at three o'clock in the morn- ing, he visited the defendant's lodging-house, and found all the beds occupied with lodgers. He again visited the house at ten o'clock, in company with Inspector Norman, when they found four bed 4 occupied by men who had been on night duty. Defendant told them that the other occupants bad got up at tour o'clock, and the four occupants they then saw went to bed about eight o'clock. The defendant knew he was breaking the by- laws as he had had them read over to him. Inspector Nortnan said on the occasion of his visit defendant was drunk, and his wife had a black eye. The defendant had been previously convicted on four charges, and he (witness) then asked for the license to be cancelled. He now asked the magistrates•ag,ain to cancel the license, as the defendant was not a fit person to be registered. Defendant was fined ss. and costs, and his license was cancelled.

A WARNING TO SHOPKEEPERS.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Monday, James Lee, 9, Old Road. Latchford, was charged with stealing from the shop of Mr. Wm. Coates, Friars-gate, on Saturday night, two pairs of men's trousers value Us. 10d.—The Chief Constable stated that on Saturday night a young man named Thomas Farringtcn was in Friars-gate when he saw the prisoner carrying something bulky and going down Ike passage near Mr. Coates' shop. Farrington went to Coates' shop and asked them if they bad missed anything. The son came forward and missed two pairs of trousers which had been hanging rutside the shop. They pursued the prisoner and he threw the trousers in a garden. P.C. Woods was informed and took the prisoner into custody: The Bench sent the pri- soner to gaol Ir one mouth hard labour.—Mr. Monks (the chairman) said the magistrates wished to impress on shopkeepers the importance of taking their goods into the shop immediately after dark. It was a temptation to many people when they saw goods exposed outside the shop after dark.—Mr. Coates said it was usual for him to have a boy watching the goads. At the time the trousers were taken, the boy was taking 301710 Of the goods into the shop.—Mr. Monka remarked that it was useless to expose goods in the dark. He noticed that a great many cases brought before the Bench Were due to this temptation. THEFT 07 SOAP FRO3I MESSRS. CROSPIELVS SOAP WORKS. At the Warrington Borough Court, yesterday, James Rider. 25, Dale-street, was charged -anith feloniously stealing from the works of Messrs. Crosflelds and Sons , Soap Works, Bank Quay, on September Ist., 56 tablets of soap valued 53., and Villiam Bincksman, of the same aldress was also charged with receiving the same know- ing it to be stolen .—lnspector Spinks said that on Thurs- day from instructions received from the Chief Constable he went to Sankey Bridges in company with P.C. Turner aud from their inquiries they got sufficient information to arrest the first defendant at the works of Messrs. Crosfields, on the charge of stealing a quantity of soap at several times but particularly on September 1. Wit- ness then went to the house of Bincksman at which the first defendant lodged, and told him that they were going to search the house. After some demur Bincksman consented. Witness found in the back bedroom, 56 tablets which weighed 21lbs. which were stamped for foreign export, and were not sold in this neighbourhood. Some soap had also ap- parently been used for washing purposes. On being charged with receiving the soap knowing it to be stolen, Bincksman said that he had warned Rider not to bring the soap to the house. Witness had called Messrs. Cros- field's attention previously to the pilferrinv of soap which was going on, and he had been instructed to watch.—Mr. J. J. Crostield said that on account of the works having so many entrances there was very great difficulty in catching anybody. They had several times been on the point ef catching people taking soap, but owing to the many entrances they had got away. The firm wished to stop this, and wished the parties to be severely dealt with although they were very sorry to have the case brought into Court.—The magistrates wished to know if the number of entrances could not be diminishee.'mt M Crosfield said that they could not, as there was a public passage through the works.—The magistrates inflicted a penalty of 10s. and costs upon each defendant or in default 11. days.

HOMOCEA-vsnsus RHEUMATISM. Hillside, Bracknell, August 3, 1393. LADY KEANE'S Compliments to the Manager, HOMOCEA COMPANY.

Kindly send me two large boxes of your Homocea and six small ones, as I find it invaluable in Rheumatism, Cuts, Bruises, kc., I may say that since using it I have suffered very little—sometimes not at all—from Rheu- matism pains in the muscles of the neck. I think so highly of it that I recommend it to my friends, also I have distributed many of the boxes you sent me amongst the poor people who are much pleased with it, and I think I can get them to take it to keep it by them in case of accident, Scc., with their children. My coach- man was bitten by one of the horses in the arm, and it turned at once quite black ; I made him rub.aome over the place, and it took away all the blackness and eased the pain. I also highly approve of it for stable use. You may make any use you like of this letter at a testimonial of its value.

HOMOCEA VERSUS PILES, Sze.

Purland Chase, Ross, July 23, 1892.

Mrs. Harvey has just had three boxes of Homocea, and she gave some to her cook to gently rub on her leg, quite a hard bunch of varicose veins and painful—what she seems to have been troubled with for a year at leJst. The solve greatly softened the hardness, and quite eased the pain. Mrs. Harvey gave a:box ot the salve to a poor woman who was suffering from protrud- ing piles, with all the accompanying giddiness, pains in the back, burning sensation at the pit of the stomach ; she really looked terribly ill. In four. days she wasn't the same person to look at, she ssems to be nearly cured, and all the pain and giddiness, &c., &c., gone, except still a little pain in the back, which will no doubt I quite leave her in a few days. RUGBY. MARRIED v. SINGLE. MATCH IN AID OF THE DISTRESS FUND.

The Warrington P. C. were left with an open date on Saturday. A match in aid of the local distress fund was arranged under the title of Married V. Single, and those who patronized the engagement had the double satisfac- tion of helping a good cause and witnessing a goocl game. Two good teams had been got together, and when Fair. Turner started operations for the married men, there was a large number of people lining the enclosure. The kick was charged, and for some time play was confined to the centre. Pretty passing saw Nevins failing to take the oval from Barnes, and Potts relieved. The juniors were having as much of the play as their opponents, and Hamblett only just cleared in time. Westall got nicely away, but dallied too long with the ball, and his kick was charged down. Boscow at this point .repeatedly proved his soundness as a custodian, but the Benedicts would not be denied, and for a considerable time play was confined to the railway end. Barnes put in some very tricky work, but to no avail, and when scoring seemed a certainty Potts intercepted a pass, and play was immediately transferred to the other end. Houghton showed up from a pack, but his pass was very wild. Monks, Edwards, and Griffiths dribbled nicely down the enclosure side, and from a line-out near the quarter-flag Griffiths gained possession and had an easy journey over, no goal resulting. The married men were chaffed by the crowd at this reverse, and the re-start saw them playing up with renewed vigour. Lally caused considerable amusement by his efforts to push Turner away on one occasion, and then some pretty passing by the Bates, Barnes, and Evans was witnessed. Westall was again prominent with a strong run down the open. and Jolley seemed all over a scorer, but was pulled down by Nevins. Barnes and Evans worked the oval away, and play was taken to the other end. Potts got in a lofty kick, but Evans met it, and running round his opponents in fine style he crossed the line near the corner flag, Turner giving the married men the lead by placing a fine goal. Half time was called with no further addition to the score. The second half was of the same character as the first, the ball travelling up and down the field very rapidly. Barnes evidently was in fine fettle, and it re- mineed one of the old days to see his tricky runs. Lally was also very prominent, but J. Bate got away from the pack and handed the ball over to Barnes. who gave the elder men a further lead by running behind the posts and _kicking a goal from the place. Then John Bate scored from a pass by James Bate, and another goal was registered by Barnes. The single men were evidently not disheartened by the heavy score against them, for after play had been located in the centre fora short time Berry got in a neat kick, and Edwards rnnning well got over, in rather a difficult position, no goal resulting. Time was now near at hand, but the scoring was not finished, for Westall got clear FL way. and ran to the line. Hamblett tackled him, but Potts was at hand and com- pleted the try, the kick at goal again failing. The whistle was now blown, and the final score read as fol- lows :—Married, three goals (15 points) ; Single, three tries (nine points).

Majority for Newton 93

ri ENTS AS FERRY BOATS.

Interesting experiments have recently been made (the Berlin correspondent of the" Standard says) with the new tents which the soldiers carry with them. 'The end in view is to make the tents, or rather their cloth, serve to construct ferry boats for the soldiers baggage. The importance of this military novelty for actual war is too great to be overlooked. Pioneers cannot always be at hand to build a bridge in cases where troops are unexpectedly obliged to cross a waterc3urse. To swim A steam in full accoutrement is not without danger even for the best swimmers. In future, however, the sol- diers will be relieved from this necessity by the folding ' tents which they carry with them. They will collect hrushwood and small branches with which to weave a light framework. The tent cloth will be unfolded, laid over the framework, and fastened to it in such a man- ner. that the water cannot pass through. The men will then strip and put all their baggage and accnuntre:nents into the sackcloth ferryboats, which will effectively pre- serve them from getting wet. The soldiers then swim across, pushing the ferryboats before them, so that they will reach the opposite shore with all their belongings dry. These experiments have been made on a large scale by the pioneers during their recently terminated autumn exercises, and have led to very favourable re- sults both as to time required and the trouble oceaskoned by the transformation. During the ma Dego vfes the ex- periments are being continued and eaten:3o to the veil- w.ts tranches of the service. •

PROPOSED BOROUGH BENCH AT

WIDNES.

A. public meeting called by advertisement. was held in the Irish National League Rooms, on Monday evening, for the purpose of considering the advisability of memorialising the Town Council of Widnes to apply to the Queen to create a borough bench of magistrates. There was a very poor attend- ance. Mr. Catuncillor Gough presided.—Mr. T. Delaney proposed : "That this meeting of burgesses of Widnes having considered the many disadvantages, delays, and unnecessary expenditure, caused through the borough having to depend upon the county magistrates for the administration of justice, and the exercise of magisterial powers, declares that a separate ' commission of the peace ought to be granted to the borough, and calls on the members of the Council tq talte immediate steps to petition Her Most Gracious Majesty the Queen to ebtain such grant."—Mr. Delaney remarked that he would not say that the present benca was not an efficient one, but he thought the business might be better conducted by a borough bench, which would be another step towards complete local self- government. A borough bench would not add anything to the rates, and they would be able to have as magis- trates, men who understood those who were brought be- fore them. The manner in which some cases had been dealt with in Widnes was a disgrace to the town, and it was time there was an alteration.—Mr. Lofthouse se- conded the motion.—Councillor O'Donnell supported. He contended that the ratepayers ought to have the right to appoint 12 or 14 magistrates froin amongst them selves. At the present time there was only one magis- trate living in the town, so that prisoners was locked up one night, and kept there sometimes until three or four o'clock next day, because the police could not get a magistrate, as the county squire was taking his ease and the commercial man was at business. They would also have to see that three or four labour mrgistrates were appointed, for it often happened that a man was tried before his employer, which was not just.—The motion was carried unanimously.

TEE COAL WAR AT EARLESTOWN. There Is is nothing new to chronicle in connection with the collier's strike so far as this district is concerned, except that the distress, which is not , alone confined to the families of Collier's, becomes more widespread and severe. The efforts however which have during the past few weeks been made to cope with it are 'still being "pushed forward, and I to some extent augmented, so that at the present time every known case of winit is being adminis- tered to. Not only does this apply to Earlestown; but cases from Collins Green, flaydock, and even on to the confines of Wigan, are receiving assistance. The inhabitants are responding very generously to the appli- cations for funds, and there does not in consequence ap- pear to be any scarcity in material from winch to pro- vide the sopp, hotpot, Ito., which is given out. le fact some of the colliers' wives say they are better fed now, than if their husband.: were at work. The Earlestown Distress Committee, continue to administer relief every day from their quarters at the Conservative Club, and on Sunday afternoon 537 children were supplied with a good meal, consisting of tea, bread and butter and buns. On Monday 90 gallons of soup and 10 loads of potatoes and 20 loaves were distlihuted, on Tuesday 200 loaves and 90 gallons of soup, and on Wednesday a similar quantity. Mr. M. Winstanley also continues his good work, and on Sunday at the Mission Hall, 340 persons partook of a substantial hot pot dinner, each child on leaving the room being presented with a bun. On Monday he gave away 100 loaves and 60 gallons of soup. Mr. Ilandyside, commencing on Monday last has entertained at the Mission Hall each day, 150 children to a substantial dinner, and 100 women to &substantial tea at five o'clock. The central relief committee is now in full working order, and have each day distributed large quantities of bread and potatoes at the Mission Hall. Were it not for these several and praiseworthy aids, it is hard to sty what would be the condition of the people at the present time. The cricket match which was played last week, realised *the sum of £l5, whicb has been handed over to the Earlestown distress committee. Another cricket match will be played on Saturday after- noon between the/' Earlestown clubs, and 22 nonrdes- cripts, when it 4s hoped a large attendance 'will be secured.
