"PUBLICANS' DIRTY WORK."

Sir,—l see in a late issee of your valuable paper, that Mr. lint, the Chief Constable of Warrindton, has given the note of %yarning to local publicans, that he and his staff in the lii' ire don't intend to do so much of the publican's dirty work as they have hitherto done. Surely Lb. put:l:cans cannot complain against the head con- stable for his statement so lately given, and so full of measin", as well aS of warning. First, it means that the punlican's business is polluted : otherwise it could net be cerrectly called "‘iirty," and, no one can disprove that fact seeorMly, it means that in the past too much of the publicans' polluted work has been done for them by our local police--at the expense of the general taxpayers who have to surport the police force ; while the waalthy brewers and publicans real) al the profits of their busineis. 'Thirdly, it shows that the gEneral tax- payers have just Eround of complaint against those who reap all the profits of the drink traffic, and saddle others with the cost, who do a more honourable and Essential business for the welfare of humanity. Fourthly. it proves ail honourable and essential trades and trades- people, ;is well as the toiling masses, are gulled and cursed by the drink traffic and its owners with the wrecks and sari effects produced by the foes of essential trade and mankind. It is an admitted fact that the drink traffic causes over fieventy-five per cent, more policemen to be employed in order to keep the pcace of the nation than would other- wise be needed. Over 360,000 persons are apprehended annually for drunkenness. In all fairness, those who reap the profit out of the hundred and forty-two millions spent annually in the drink trade ought to bear all the expense of their "dirty work," and even then, the sober public would have to endure vastly too much of personal insult and personal injury arising out of the publican's " dirty work." About three hundred thousand persons are annually confined in the workhouses in this country, while 3,000,000 persons come upon the books of the , parish unions, mostly the "dirty work" of the publicans, and about 80,000,000 bushels of grain destroyed annually in the "dirty work" of the publicans. To crown all the above, 120,000 deaths are annually caused, either directly or indirectly, to perpetuate the " dirty work "of the publicans ; in addition to untabulated and , unutterable pains and miseries endured by our sisters and brothers. Those who reap the cash blessings of the drink traffic and those who have legalised the traffic and dabble in the glass, are so in love with the publican's work, and with their own views thereon, that they glory in sup- posing themselves the only perfect examples worthy of imitation,and the only perfect judges and rulers "of the publican's dirty work." They sneer at all those who abstain, for conscience sake, as bigots unfit to betaken any notice of. But tieeper, and broader reflection shows that wine bibbers and those who reap £ s. d. out of the drink traffic are actuated by selfishness only. Whereas, those who live and labour without either strong drink or the £ s. d. profit thereon, spend and are being spent for the general good of their fellow-creatures and the honour of humanity. How long shall the most selfish and heartless class of tradespeople pride themselves and their friends as if they were the people whose chief aim in their traffic was to bless mankind, while all the facts of the case proves that their traffic is, as St. Augustine states, "The mother of all mischief, the root of crimes, the spring of vices, the whirlwind of the brain, the tempest of the tongue, the ruin of the body, the wreck of chastity, a loss of time, a voluntary rage, a shameful weakness, the stain of honesty, and plague and corruption of the soul ?"—Faithfully yours, R. STOREY,

TIIE COAL CRISIS.

Sir,—ln the interests of suffering humanity—of hungry men, famishing women, and starving children —can nothing be devised to put an end to this terrible tragedy now being enacted between the colliery pro- prietors and their workpeople ; for it is nothing other than a tragedy, when men are being shot down and children, and possibly adults, are dying of hunger. A touching case was reported from Cumberland in last Saturday's "Manchester Guardian." A child of two years of age died ot starvation ; the father in despera- tion said he would not see his family starve any longer —he would go to work and risk it. He did go. 'I bat night he was savagely beaten, his family maltreated, his furniture smashed ,and his windows and house wrecked. One would scarcely believe a few weeks ago that such things would happen as have taken place in the interval —acts that place the perpetrators beyond the pale of civilisation and consign them to barbarism. Acts that tend to alienate the sympathy that has been extended

to the men on strike 'fron the commencement. to now,

and will. I hope be still extended in a practical form - until this war of interests shall be ended. But it must be borne in mind that many other interests and busi- nesses are brought to a stand besides the miners, and there is much suffering and privation all round, thus curtailing the help that would otherwise be given. One thing can be done if nothing else, and that i 3 to refrain, as far as possible, from the use of exasperating lan- guage. Both sides are hurting fierce defiance at each other. This does no good, as moderate language is quita as powerful and effective as the fiercestrailing that tongue can utter er pen write. A teaspoonful of oil will go further in allaying friction than a pint of vinegar. I think it would be wisdom and no loss of prestige if the masters were to hold out the flag of truce. They were the first to declare war by demanding a reduction of 25 per cent, in wages. And there is an explanation wanting here. While some say that it is 25 per cent. on current rates, others say 25 per cent, off the rates of 1888 ; others that it is 25 per cent, off the advances of 40 per cent, given since 1888. Which is it? or what is the actual reduction demanded? Be it how it may, 25 per cent, is a big drop, and should be plainly stated. It has been said that vast fortunes have been made in coal. So there has, in many other businesses—in beer for instance. The drink manufacturers pile up their , millions and few comparatively strike against them. Fortunes have been lost in coal. I know of many, and but for encroaching too much on your space I would name them. I mention one, it was a co-operative con- cern near Wakefield. It collapsed when coal was double the price it is now. There are prosperous and profitless collieries. The royalty owner (who never stirs a finger except to receive that which he never earned) stands first on the list to profit by coal mining. After him the middle man (especially in the house coal trade). Coal doubles in price from the pit hank to the coal cellar of , the consumer, and neither the colliery proprietor nor the miner gets any of this plunder. I commenced this letter with an earnest wish that this gad and terrible state of things may be amicably settled, as far as the attendant excitement will permit, in the in- terests of peace and prosperity that the hungry may be fed and that the cruel starvation may be ended. The masters are supposed to be more intelligent than the workmen. Undoubtedly they have more influence. Let them use the intelligence that superior education and extensive reading and observation, combined with the influence which wealth and social position gives them, and by friendly conference with the leaders of the men devise some means of ending this fraticidal war and rescue the paraLzed industry of the country from the depths to which it has fallen and from the fake Raition in which the whole of the commerce of the nation is now placed —Yours respectively, Sankey bridges, Sep. 13, 1893.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON.

TEIE F.S.A. AT WYCLIFFE CHURCEI. The vestibule of Wycliffe Church had quite an ani- mated Appearance on Sunday afternoon, the reason laving that 375 members of the P.& A. society were receiving their prizes for regular attendance during the firat half- year. The books which were piled up on tables were distributed by the stewards. They comprised an excel- lent and varied selection of the works of popular writers. 61 certificates of merit were also given. The attendance

of members was consequently very large. The chair was occupied by Councillor Beaman who delivered a short address, contrasting the Sabbath observance of 100 years ago with that of the present time. The address was delivered by Councillor Trevor, of Manchester, who based his remarks on the text " The trees are full of sap" Psalm 104. The musical part of the meeting, com- prised an instrumental selection, and a solo by Miss N. Roberts, which were well rendered. The meeting was also taken part in by the Rev. J. Yonge, Messrs. J. Pod- more, and D. Owen. The membership now reaches nearly 800.

ATHLETICS.

NOTiS ON G 9 INIE6 D

CRICKET.

Warrington first team concluded their fixtures for this season by what was practically a disastrous defeat, as Sale scored 156 for the loss of only three wickets, while Warrington with only one wicket to fall had only made 55. Of course Warrington had only a very poor team with S. M. and J. J. Crosfield, R. Smith, rodmore, Voisey, and Mortimer absent, but still the difference in the two scores is rather too much to be explained away on that ground. C. H. Benton, the Lancashire county player gave a very brilliant display for his 38, as also did Whittle who made 56 not out. On the Warrington side Unsworth with 26, and Jeans with 14 are the only scores worth mentioning. There were no less than five " ducks." Ashton with two wickets for 11 runs was the most successful Warrington bowler, and the way in which Sale scored is well shown that although they only lost three wickets it took five bowlers to do it.

With the fixtures with Sale, Warrington have finished up a very poor season. It has been the worst the team has known for a long time, some of their defeats being simply beyond comprehension as witness their defeat at Warrington by Birkenhead Park. Out of the 23 matches played. they have only won six, drawn five, and have lost twelve.

Warrington 2nd team were more successful than their seniors on Saturday, for they managed to pull off the match with Sale by a victory of 89 for nine wickets to 78. This is a welcome change, and just balances their wins and losses as they have won eight, lost eight, and drawn three. Hampson with 20 not out was top scorer for Warrington. Warrington's scoring was more regu• tar than Sale as four got into double figures, which on the other hand, only one of the Sale team, Hamilton with 26 could do much with the bowling.

With last week's enga;,ements we must now say good- bye to cricket. The season which has just closed has been an exceptional one. Rain which has spoiled so many matches in other seasons has this time given cricketers a chance, and the result has been that for the first time two batsmen viz., Gunn and A. E. Stoddart, I have made over 2,000 runs in first class cricket. Only one has previously gained this distinction and that was the player who has made so many records, W. G. Grace. As regards Warrington cricket, there has been an enormous number of clubs im the town, and some very good cricket has been played. Let us hope that the first eleven will have a better record next season than this,however.

The Penketh Friends C.C. finished a very successful season on Saturday, winning 21 matches and only losing one. Smith heads both bowling and batting averages, in the former taking 75 wickets at an average of 1-73 runs per wicket, and having a batting average of 3-5 for 19 innings. Linney, the captain, comes secona in the bat- ting averages with 30.8 for 18 innings.

The second eleven have not had many matches during the season, the total number being six,out of which they won four, lost one, and drew one.

FOOTBALL.

The Warrington footballers were acting the part of the good Samaritan in caring for the poor on Saturday. Having no fixture, a match was arranged between the bachelors and benedicts of the club,and the proceeds were devoted to the fund for the relief of the distress in the town. A good number of spectators were present, and saw a good game for their money. The benedicts were fortunate in having a good goal kicker, for it was by this means that they secured the victory, as they got three goals while the juveniles got three tries, which were not converted. Boscow, as the bachelor full back, made his first appearance before a Warrington " gate " this season, and showed that he had lost none of his coolness, and his value as acustodian which won him county honours last year. I wonder if Lally thought he was Fair. Turner's match when he at- tempted to push him away at one time during the match? It looked like a giant and dwarf set-to. The married men were fortunate in the fact that Jack and Smack Bate were married, for these two players contri- buted not a little towards the result.

The Warrington "A" were also without a fixture, and some of its players were engaged in the internecine strife which was waged on the Wilderspool ground on Saturday afternoon, so that the only team belonging to the W.F.C. in "foreign parts" was the second team, who were antagonising Pendleton Trinity first at Pendle- ton A very pleasant game was played, and the result was also pleasant to Warrington, for they won by 14 points to nil, the contributors to this being Wilcock, whose share was two tries, and Carey and Gibson, who got a try each. Gibson also placed the only goal.

Warrini,ton defeated St. Helens Recs. at St. Helens on Thursday by two tries to a try, a feat they have not performed away from home for a great number of years. The match was a very peculiar one, each skit, scoring a try at the first few minutes of the game, but Wi.-ring,ton managed to outstay their opponents and scored again. Coming as this doas after the manner in which the RiICS. played against Swinton on Saturday when neither side could score, it is very satisfactory from a iVarrington point of view.

The Lancashire County Rugby Football Club have de- clared in favour of the payment to players for loss of time occasioned by thelaying of matches, by 47 votes to 35. The president, Mr. A. N. Hornby spoke very strongly in favour of the payment of the players, but Mr. J. H. Payne, the secretary for the past year, was as strongly against it., each side claiming that they were guarding against professionalism. However, both Lan- cashire and Yorkshire have now declared in favour of the proposal, and it is probable that it will be adopted in Rugby circles.—Mr. Warren, of Warrington has been re-elected on the committee for the selection of refer- ees. Mr. J. H. Payne declined re-election as secretary and has been succeeded by Mr. A. M. Crooke.

St. Mary's team paid a visit to Wigan ma Saturday, to meet the Recreation club of that town, and returned to Warrington victors by a try to none. The try was got by means of Thomas and Flampson. the latter scoring under the posts, but the kick at goal failed. There was some rough play in the latter half, but the Recreation could only get three minors, which,of course, do not count.

The second team of St. Mary's also won their seeond victory on Satuiday, over the Wigan Recreation 2nd. After a good game the result was that St. Mary's had got over the line twice to their opponents once.

The junior clubs of the town have not yet begun their season in earnest, but the Rangers commenced theirs on Saturday last, when they paid a visit to Widnes to meet the Rangers of that town. The two rival Rangers appear to be unable to beat each other, however, for, in the third successive match, they scored nothing tangible. This should surely prove that the two teams are pretty nearly equal.

The Penketh Friends school have a very heavy pro- gramme before them in the present football season as they have already arranged 44 matches. By the time they have got through this, they will surely have had their fill of football.

A meeting of the Warrington District Football Asso- ciation was held on Monday evening, Mr. W. Gould, of the Lymm A.F C. eccupying, the chair. Mr. J. Mather, the hon. sec., read the reply from the Football Associa- tion with reference to the affiliation of clubs, associa- tions, &c., and it was decided to affiliate the Warrington Ass4ciation with Liverpool and district, a radius of 12 miles being agreed upon. A sub-committee of five member; was appointed to frame a code of rules of management, subject to the next meeting, and the annual subscription was fixed at ss. for each club.

The Penketh A.F.C., under the captaincy of Mr. J. Elliot,have opened their season with an excellent victory of five goals to nil over Broadheath away from home. They have a very good team, and if they do themselves justice they will give a gooci game to any club in the district.

CYCLING.

Sixteen members of the Warrington Cycling Club went on their weekly run on Saturday. They went round by Hon Green, Tabley, Plumbley, to Knutsford where a good tea was provided. They arrived home at abcut ten o'clock in the evening after a very agreeable ride.

As the seascn of cycling is almost over, the night runs have been stopped. The club is making great prepara- tion for the winter. They are anine to have a soiree late in October, and two er three " smokers " will alQo he ar- ranged for the winter months. It is also probable that a lantern exhibition will be held showing different cycling resorts, ecc. There are now only three more fi xtores this season so that all members who wish to take part in them this year must look sharp.

A REVISION COURT INCIDENT. SALARIES IN THE BREWING TRADE.

NEWS FOR THE SURVEYOR OF TAXES. At the Warrington Revision Court, on Tuei,day, a young man,natned Ernest Parry, made a claim, un- der Conservative auspices, for a lodger's vote. He said he lodged at a house in Froghall-lane, and paid £3O a year for the use of his rooms. As the rent of the house is only some £l4 or £l6 a year,there was clearly an opEning for cross-examination by Coun- cillor Roberts, who represented the Liberals. One of the questions asked was as to the income of a claimant who paid so liberally for his rooms. This question was not answered, but still it will be seen from the following correspondence that it has elicited some very interesting information as to the salaries that are paid, and the epistolary etiquette that prevails, in the "brewing trade." By an early post Councillor Roberts received an unstamped envelope (charge 2d.) addressed to Mr. " Harry " Roberts,and containing,besides the letter given below,one of the price lists of ales,porter, and stout of Messrs. P. Walker and Son, on which were written the words 'No reduction to teetotallers." The letter, which is written on one of Messrs. Walker's memorandum forms, reads as follows : "My dearest Roberts,—! omitted to give you my in- come this morning, but you see being in the brewing trade it is naturally a good one. After a little calcula- tion I find it just under £lO,OOO a year.—Yours very sincerely, ERNEST PARRY."

Mr. Roberts' reply is in the following terms : Warrington, Sep. 13, 1233.—5ir,—1 Rill duly in receipt of yours of 12th inst. Not having had the pleasure (to say nothing of the honour?) of your acquaintance, prior to meeting you in the Revision Court, I am somewhat puzzled at the terms of endearment arid familiarity with which you address me. No doubt it is satisfactory to you to be the recipient of so large an income (although as to its source there may be two opinions),and in order that you may contribute your equitable share to the Imperial Exchequer, probably the Surveyor of Taxes will be glad to see that you are properly assessed. As you grow older, and presumably wiser, you will realise the fact, that it is often tl,e duty of an advocate to put questions to hostile witnesses which are not particularly pleasant to the latter, and as it was in that capacity only that I asked the question which seems to have dis- turbed you, I have no excuse further than this explana- tion, to offer in regard to it. As you cannot plead poverty, and as no doubt you wish to be considered a gentleman, I must ask you to send me the sum of twopence which I have had to pay, for your omission to put a penny postage stamp upon the letter you sent me. Thinking that this correspondence may be somewhat in- teresting to the public. I purpose sending it to the local press —Believe me, sir, yours truly, HENRY HOMERTS.— Mr. Ernest Parry, Messrs. Peter Walker and Son, Limited, Warrington.

THE PUBLIC-HOUSES OF WARRINGTON.

INIPORTANT MAGISTERIAL REPORT.

The Warrington magistrates held a private meet- ing on Monday afternoon at the Town Hall to dis- cuss the back door question. Mr. John Crosfield occupied the chair. After about two hours' dis- cussion it was decided that all publicans be served with notices to close the back doors formerly used for the purposes of trade. This decision will effect all liquor sellers both off and on licenses.

The following report was presented to the meeting : Town Hall, September Bth, 1893. Gentlemen,—ln pursuance of the resolution passed at the meeting in the Town Hall, on Monday, the 4th inst., we have personally inspected the houses marked "A " and " B" in the list submitted by the Chief Constable. We find his description quite correct in every case, and we suggest that he be requested to keep it corrected, as it will form a very satisfactory basis for future reference. We think the most satisfactory way will be for the Bench' to consider fully, and very carefully, the future policy to be adopted, and to settle what are to be the conditions , in future for the renewal of a license which shall be equitable and reasonable upon the owners and yet pro- tect the public interests. The result of our examination ' is to convince us that police supervision cannot be effective unless and until every full-licensed house is self-contained ; that is, that the occupier has entire con- trol of his own premises, that no other tenant has access to any private passage, yard or other premises. That off-licensed houses shall not use any side or back door for other than domestic purposes, that is, that only the front entrance to any off-licensed house shall be used for trade purposes. Although not asked to visit the beerhouses, we thought it desirable te visit a few of them, and the condition of those we did visit revealed a state of things it is most desirable to alter, and we suggest that the attention of the Home Office be called to the desirabilty of placing beerhouses under the same condi- tions as fully licensed houses in respect of the granting and renewal of their licenses. If tliese conditions are accepted for future guidance, the management and con- trol of this business will be very much easier, and will , not unduly interfere with the legitimate trader who iseeks to conduct his business in an honest and honour- able way. Another fact which we noticed was the large number of off licenses in the poorer districts of the town, and in some districts they are so close together as to suggest that they were far in excess of the legitimate requirements of the population around them. We also call attention to a practice of many license holders of giving coppers and sweets to young children as an inducement for increasing trade; alsn to the tendency of off licenses to become places for the sale of beer only. We folly appreciate the difficulties whicn owners and occupiers'will have in adapting themselves to these con- ditions, if adopted ; at the same time they are mainly difficulties of a character which a judicious expenditure 11 will overcome, if time be granted for the necessary alterations. Where the owners will not incur the neces- sary outlay, it will he for you to determine what is best in the public interests.—Yours faithfully,

MAN BURIED ALIVE IN THE SHIP CANAL.

NARROW ESCAPE.

On Tuesday evening, about six o'clock, an acci- dent occurred on the Ship Canal at Stockton Heath, by which one man, James Butler, was seriously injured, and several others had a most narrow escape of their lives. • On the Latchford side of the swing bridge on the Stockton Heath-road, where the sides of the canal are very sandy, a number of men were engaged in clearing the earth from ! around a large piece of rock, which it. was intended to remove by blasting. Unluckily, they had worked in such a manner as to cause the bank to assume a perpendicular shape, instead of clearing away the top, and keeping it on a slope. The result was that the sand, lacking in adhesiveness, suddenly fell in a mass of several tons, and descending upon the only man who happened to be tinder it at the time, I hurled him to the bottom of the canal, where he lay completely buried under it. His mates being near at hand, at once set to work to effect a rescue. This they were not long in doing, and the man was extricated in an unconscious state. He was removed to 'the hospital at Latchford, when it was found that his injuries were of a grave nature. He however, progressing very satisfactorily.

TESTIMONIAL TO Mil. WM. OWEN, J.P.

The School of Art has become a very flourishing institution in Warrington and to a great measure this has been due to the praiseworthy exertions of Mr. William Owen who has been the secretary for the past twelve ye.ars. The Committee thought that it would be very fitting to recognise his ener- getic and hearty exertions on behalf of the school, and after discussing the matter it was determine to make him a testimonial. On Wednesday a dual meeting of the Committee was called—the bchool having now been transferred to the Corporation— at which a beautiful silver tea service, consisting of a teapot, kettle, cream jug. sugar basin, spoons, &c., and silver salver, enclosed in a handsome mahogany case, was presented to Mr. Owen on behalf of the Committee by Mr. John Rylands, J.P., the chair- man of the Committee. Mr. Rylands very appro- priately alluded to Mr. Owen's faithful and untir- ing zeal in connection with the school, and said that he had contributed largely to its success. Mr. Owen replied by expressing his heartfelt thankful- ness to the Committee for the testimonial, and said that he was unworthy of it, but he had done the work he was called upon to do with pleasure, and the work he bad done he believed was in the in- terest of the town. The inscription on the service was as follows: "Presented to Mr. Vv m. Owen, J.P., by the Warrington Schonl of Art, in acknowledg- ment of his long and faithful services as secretary and treasurer of the institution."

FAILURE OF MAJOR CROSS OF WIDNES.

On Tuesday, ot the Wrexham Bankruptcy Court, Major Tomes Cross, civil engineer by profession,and at present residing at Eirianfa, Llangollen, came up for his public examination. Mr. J. Hepley Pierce appeared for the London and Universal Bank. The statement of affairs showed gross liabilities amounting to £35,105 17s. 6d., but of these only £10,591 103. were expected to rank. The assets were put down at £77 ss. 7d., leaving a deficiency of £10,591 103.—The official receiver(Mr. L. Hugh Jones) stated that the debtor was, from 1865 to August 8, 1893, managing trustee to the Hutchinson estate, Widnes, and from 1864 to 1868 he traded as a locomotive builder at St. Helens, under the style of James Cross and Co. In the latter year ha became insolvent, the smaller creditors of the estate being paid in full, and the larger ones receiving 10s. in the pound. The debtor's life is insured for £9OOO but the policies are held as securities by Parr's Bank, Limited, and toe Widnes and Ditton Land Company The debtor was possessed of freehold and leasehold land at Widnes, but it had been taken possession of by the Liverpool Investment Society. The debtor alleged his failure to be due to his having become surety far a Mr. J. W. Weight, a London stockbroker, for £8,909. He considered that he had a claim against the estate of the late John Hutchinson, which was now in I Chancery, for_.£l6,ooo. He had given 28 years of his life to the making of this estate, and had been turned ! out at a moment's notice, and a receiver put in by the Court of Chancery. The land taken possession of by the Liverppol Investment Society was 53 acres, aud was Purchased with other land when he made the West Widnes Railway for the Midland and Manchester, Sheffield. and Lincolnshire Railway Companies. The line was made without an Act of Parliament, and to get the land for the line he had to buy other land to induce the owners to sell at all: He undertook to make the line for the railway companies for a lump sum, and the speculation wasi a profitable one. The IVidnes land had been valued at £21,000, but probably it would not rea- lize more than £15,000. He lost £B,OOO in the locomo- tive works, and £6,500 in the Composite Ore Company, Limited, a company formed for the purpose of extract- ing silver and lead from the Welsh composite nres. He became surety for Weight to the London and Universal Bank. He had no security for the first advance oh £5.000. as he was not then aware that Weight was in difficulties. He had always believed him to be a wealthy man. Weight promised to buy the Widnes land for £25,000, but when he was asked to sign the contract lie stated that he was in difficulties. He had since I failed, and there were no assets.—The examination was adjourned to October 10.

NEW RELIEF SCHEME FOR THE UNEMPLOYED.

The Central News learns that Sir John Gorst, in view of the necessity which may arise next winter of provid- ing relief works for the unemployed in London, has been in private communication with the President of the Local Government Board in reference to a scheme which lie thinks might be adopted experimentally. Sir John's proposal is to acquire provisionally some of the waste land lying in the eastern quarter of London and have it broken and worked up for agricultural purposes under the direction ot the workhouse authorities. The persons thus Qmployed would not enter into competition with the regular town industries, a thing which Sir John Gorst is strongly opposed to, but simply be occupied in adding to the food resources of the country. Power is given under some old unrepealed Poor Law Acts to over- seers to rent fifty acres of land in each union, and to set upon it to work at reasonable remuneration the unem- ployed of the parish. The authority is now vested in the Local Government Board, but Mr. Fowler has replied that he covld not sanction any such aNuisition without his colleagues, and it would therefore be necessary to make it a Cabinet question.

THE PASTORATE OF HILL CLIFFE BAPTIST CHAPEL. The Rev. Thomas Samuel Hughes, Baptist minis- ter now of Earlestown, and late of Bacup, has now definitely decided to accept the call of the pastorate of 11111 Uliffe Baptist Chapel. The rev. gentleman intimated his acceptance on Sunday. Mr. Hughes will commence his ministry at Hill Cliffe. on the third Sunday lit October. THE WOREINGMF:N'S GOSPEL TEMPERANCE MISSION. On Friday night last, a pleasant meeting was held in the Workingmen's Gospel Temperance Mission Hall, when Mr. Storey read a passage from the second Epistle of Peter. Messrs. Drury, Blythe, Horrocks, and Rigby joined in thd conversation which followed. The subject was adjourned. On Saturday night, an open air meeting was held at the end of Bewsey-street, conducted by Mr. R. Storey. The principal speaker for the occasion was Mr. Lloyd Young of Manchester,who delivered a stirring address. Mr. J. Drury also addressed the meeting, a number of persons signed the pledge. On Sun- day night another meeting was held at the same place, conducted by Mr. J. Drury. Messrs. J. Tan- ner. J. Rigby, Horrocks, Iligginson, H. Taylor, P.M.C.. and H. Storey also addressed the meet- ing, a large crowd listened very attentively, two hours. The meeting was a great success. On Monday night last a well attended Band of Hope meeting was held in the Temperance Hall, singing, reciting, &c., were much enjoyed.

WARRINGTON SLAUGHTER

===== 0002601_18930916_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
HOUSES.

V.-THE PLEADINGS OF HUMANITY.

(By "CLEo.")

The demands of health and the slairns of morality having been heard, the pleadings of humanity now Crave a hearing also. Man is a carnivorous animal, therefore, it is in the nature of things that the beasts of the field, birds of the air, and fish of the sea should be sacrificed on the altar of his appetite, but as long as there is any humanity in man every care should be taken that that appetite is gratified with the least possible pain andsufferingtoits victims Tune was when acts of the grossest cruelty in this direction were openly perpetrated without let ot hindrance. Happily, such times are past and gone, but it would be extremely imprudent to imagine that all such acts of unnecessary cruelty have gone with them. Indeed the residenters in the immediate neighbourhood of slaughter-houses, particularly : those adjoining slaughter-house premises,are strong in their belief that unnecessary cruelty is carried ' on. Take for instance, the veal season. It is well known that to reduce the flesh of calves to the whiteness that gives it its high market value, iS was formerly the recognised practice to cut an ar- tery in the calf's neck and let it bleed until it dropped down and expired through sheer exhaus- tion. Sometimes the poor beast would linger as long as twe days or more. I understand that the Royal Seciety for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals has recently stepped in and rendered anyone guilty of this practice amenable to the law. An apparently singular anomaly, however, exists, as I doubt whether an officer of that society could claim a locus standi or right of entrance to a private slaughter house either to make investigations or to verify suspicions, although the refusal to grant him admission would be a strong element of sus- picion against the occupier. lam sorry to say that there is a strong impression amongst residenters in the immediate vicinity of slaughter houses that this practice or one closely akin to it is not wholly absent in Warrington. Of this there is not the faintest shadow of a doubt that during the veal season the calves within the slaughter houses keep up a most piteous bleating all night through as if in agony and pain, and in such a way as to give very feasible grounds for the opinion that the calves are hung up by the hind heels and with their arteries opened are allowed to bleed to death. When one remembers that the Christmas turkey was usually put to death in the same manner, viz., first having its tongue cut out and then being hung up by the legs until it bled to death, more credence is given to the ktatement that calves are yet subjected to the same treatment. However sincere the people may be in this belief, I rather think that they are under a misapprehension, as cases of habitual and prolonged cruelty would be sure to come under the notice of the police who have a perfect right to enter the slaughter houses where they have any suspicions of cruelty being practised. Speaking of this species of torture reminds me of the tortuous process by which the Jews put bullocks to death, a function which is only performed by their Chief Rabbi. This however is a, religious ceremony and therefore outside legislative interference. The case of bleeding calves to death is only a par- ticular instance. As to the general question of un- necessary cruelty there can be no doubt that na.uch is done that ought not to be done, and much is left undone that ought to be done. No. 8 of the Cor- poration byelaws—" No animal to be subjected to any unnecessary pain "—seems to be the only item on paper dealing with the question of cruelty. This would be sufficient if there were any specific provision made to see that this bye- law was carried out, but as has already been pointed out no such provision exists even for the adequate supervision of the slaughter houses in any shape or form, let alone the particular duty of'pre- venting or detecting any undue cruelty. The story in our school-books of the butcher and theelsildron'a pet lamb, wherein be says that although lie was a butcher ha had no harder heart than any other men was one which touched our boyish emotions, brit fuller years teach us that this particular butcher must have been a rare swig of his species so far as the immediate exercise of their calling is con- cerned. Familiarity breeds contempt consequently their necessary familiarity with the scenes of the slaughter houses, and the part they take in them must of necessity blunt their sympathies with the sufferings of the dumb animals, and as the feeling of being accustomed to it grows upon there the feeling that their victims also become accustomed to it grows with it much the same way, as it was in the case with the fisherwoman who whet: asked how she could be so cruel as to skin the eels alive replied " Oh, the creatures are accustomed to it." Here then is a wide field of opportunity to dotricks of the trade that have a tendency to increase rather than diminish pain, and to omit precautions which would tend to alleviate the sufferings of the victims. There is doubtless an honest rivalry between many of these hardworking men to outdo their neigh- bours in the celerity by which they get through their work, but even in this otherwise desirable rt - commendation there lurks a considerable danger that the feelings of the objects operated upon are overlooked. It is not in the cases of the old experi- enced hands that this danger is mostly to be feared it is the apprentices and beginners. On busy oc- casions there is not time to superintend the begin- ner's operations, and the result is that he does not go the right way about it, and sometimes has had to leave it half done, the finishing touches being perfermed by older hands to the consequent prolonging and intensifying of suffering. But it is worse still to allow any on- looker with not the rsmotest idea of how to do it properly to tty his 'prentice hand at the business, and instances of this are numerous. To do the work expeditiously and properly requires a steady hand and at least a rudimentery knowledge of anatomy. A bullock should always be felled and stunned by one blow, and the " pithing " which follows should be the work of a second or two." Pithing" consists in inserting a flexible wire rod into the opening made by the pole-axe on the forehead, and then ramming it as far as it will reach inside the vertebra column. This process immediately puts the animal out of all suffering although it may struggle for a few minutes afterwards. In one of the slaughter-houses visited one beast had to receive three distinct blows. This was looked upon as being nothing at all unusual. It is not only in the process itself or at the hands of apprentice, unskilled or reckless workmen that undue cruelty is exercised, a more refined cruelty is carried on under circumstances which allow of no extenuation. I have already referred to the beastly state of some of the stalls and sheep-pens on the Warrington slaughter-house premises. Can any- thing be more cruel than to have a score or more sheep cooped up in a low, dark, ill-ventilated cellar- looking place in full view of what is going on, and of the fate that is in store for them, and without either food or water? To realize to what extent this is done one has but to see it, and this one can easily do without walking a mile from Market Gate. It is much the same with the cattle, with this addition that in their violent struggles when being led to the killing shed they are often be- laboured unmercifully. The beasts appearing to know instinctively what fate Is awaiting them ' sometimes flop down across the doorway and have practically to be be hauled into position, so violent are their struggles. All this and much more would be prevented were a properly constructed and arranged abattoir substituted for the present badly constructed and worse arranged buildings. Add to the present drawbacks the fact that characters such as the " bungsucker " I have described are employed as jobbers. What tender- ness is to be expected from the regular loafers, cor- nermen,and general riff raff men in slaughterhouses, more bestial than the beasts themselves. Fortu- nately this it apparently the exception in Warring- ton, but that it is done in some cases and could be done in all is sufficient warrant to arrest the present state of things and consign them into the everlast- ing limbo of dead and forgotten things.

PICNICS TO FRODSHAM AND SOUTHPORT. The members of the Ragged Sunday school band and 22 girls met at the school on Saturday, and received a good dinner, after which the boys pro- ceeded to Frodsham and the girls to Southport, where they were provided with excellent teas. After spending a pleasant afternoon they reached home about 10 p.m.

DEATH OF A PULICAN. , Mr. John Pritchard, landlord of the Griffin Hotel died on Wednesday morning, at the age of 62 years Death is stated to be due to a general breaking up of the system. Ile had been ailing for several months, but only took to his bed a few days ago. The deceased gentleman had resided at the Griffin for many years, and was well known and highly respected, not only among the townspeople, but by the whole of the farming community round about Wan rington.
